About a fortnight since I left my work—l commenced my illness by feeling starved, and a nasty stinging and shooting pain in my head; a doctor was called in, and he said it was the Influenza, and I sufftred for over a week of the pains in my head, and the doctor's medicine did mono good, till at last the mistress of this house (I am in lodgings here) fell ill with the same symptoms of illness till her husband advised us to try a bottle of your Bitters. He got a is. lid. bottle, and I may tell you the first &se gave us relief. After this we got a 28. 9d. bottle, and now we are all right and I am at my work now, and I have no doubt that if I had taken the Bitters at first I should have lost no work, and I owe my recovery to the bene- ficial virtues of your medicine, and I beg to tender te you my sincere thanks for producing such a valuable medicine to suffering humanity. SENT

'an SON OF A LATE WARRINGTON ALDERMAN GAOL. At the Borough Police Court on Friday, before Alder- man Wright (chairman). Dr. Gornall, and Councillor H. Roberts, Richard Kean, 26, Leonard-st, and son of the lase Alderman Kean, was charged with having on *Thursday, March 7, assaulted and beaten his wife,Maria Rean.—Mrs. Kean, the complainant, a respectable look- ing person, stated that on Wednesday night defendant mane home about 12 o'clock, and on Thursday morning he oommenced to assault her. He broke the pictures in the house which were her property and not his. He had struck her daughter, and complainant had had no comfort with him for years. She had eight children and wished for a separation order.—Maud Kean, daughter of cormaainant Stated that the defendant kicked her mother on the arm and in trying to prevent him she was struck herself.— Defendant stated that his wife had emptied the contents of a teapot upon him and they had bad a scuffie.—P.C. Barlow said he apprehended the prisoner on Thursday evening about 5.30, and he was drunk then. Witness had also visited th 3 house of complainant and found all the pictures broken .—Defendant was nitimately sent to gaol for ene month's hard labour, and complainant was granted a separation order with ss. a week allowance, 'which she said she would be satisfied with. A SUSPICIOUS CHARACTER UNDER SUSPICIOUS CIRCUMSTANCES. At the Warrington Borough Police Court, on Monday, before the Mayor and other magistrates, William Finch, 56, Pinner's Brow, was charged with having on the 3rd March been found on the enclosed premises of Messrs. Armitage and Rigby, Orford-street, for all unlawful purpose or with the intent to commit a felony.—Mr. Browne, solicitor, appeared to prosecute on behalf of Messrs. Armitage and Rigby and said that formerly the defendant was in the employ of the firm and left some years ago. Since Mr.llibbert had been the manager he had occasionally taken him on and since his discharge the man had persistently entered the premises of the works,over the railway and they were afraid something dangerous might occur. Some three weeks ago the manager met him on the premises and told him he would he locked up if he came again. Last Sunday, the watchman,Wileock, went to one of the boilers for the purpose of turning on steam and Ritmo the defendant on top of it. At the time there was 180 pounds steam pressure up and the man bad only to turn one tap to blow up the whole place The man was a nuisance and would persist in com- ing on the premises, and on another occasion he was caught there during a mealtime. Mr. Brovrne suggested that the defendant be bound over for judgment when called upon or remanded.—Alderman Harrison to defen- dant: Do you smoke? Defendant: Sometimes, but I don't carry matches.—Mr. Wilcock, fireman, in the employ of Messrs. Armitage and Rigby, said that at the time he found defendant on top of the boiler the latter said he had come to have a lie down. Witness had never found him interfering with the boilers at all. Defend- ant, bethought, came in over the railway.--11 r. Hibbert, the manager, after corroborating Mr. Browne, stated that he, on one occasion, met the defendant on the premises during a meal time and asked him what ha wanted. Defendant said that he wanted to see Wilcock, but witness had since found that Wilcock had nothing to do with the defendant and therefore the latter could not have wanted to see him. Witness had had complaints from the night-watchman about defendant and once the latter was found drying his clothes at the boiler as he had tumbled into a reservoir in getting over the wall.— ' Defendant owned to being on the premises, but denied that he was there to commit a felony or do any damage at all. When questioned as to how he got out of the reservoir which is sft. deep, defendant said he ought to be able to swim as he had been to the bottom many a time to open the pipes.—lle was remanded for a inonth and warned that if caught on the premises again he would be locked up.

TtrESDAY.—Before Mr. Robert Garnett (in the chair), Mr. E. J. Guest and Dr. Gornall. ANOTHER ASSAULT ON THE POLICE.--WilliaM HE nry iForest, a naiddle-aged man, was charged with being an idle and disorderly person, and having been found begg- ng at eight o'clock tho previous night in Sankey-5t He was also charged with having assaulted Detective Monaghan and P.C. Roper while being brought to the Police Station.—Detective Monaghan stated that from information he received about eight o'clock the prdvious night, he went to Sankey-at., and saw the prisoner near to Messrs. Dutton's shop. There was also another man on the opposite side of the road. They were both drunk and begging. When anyone refused to give them any- thing they used bad language. On witness going to pri- soner he became very violent, and began to butt at him with his head. P.C. Roper came to his assistance. Pri- soner was very violent at the Police Station. He said he could do a bit of " purring." Witness thought he had come from Wigan. There were about eight of this class ot men in the town.—P.C. Roper gave corrobora- tive evidence.—Prisoner was sent to gaol for 14 days +with hard labour. The defendant then conwnenced to flourish his stick to frighten her,and in dieing so damaged a chest of drawers. fie also got hold of Mrs. Ellam by the shoulders, and knocked her head against the wall and otherwise abused her.—Evidence in support of Mr. Longton's statement was given by the complainant, and also Martha Ellam, her mother-in-law, who was present at the time the struggle took place.—Mr. R. SELLING DILUTED Rum Margaret Blundell, ' Newton, was charged with selling a pint ef rum which was 32 per cent, under proof, it only being allowed 25 per cent, under proof.—Detective Carleton said that he bought the rum at the defendant's house on February 14th, and then divided it, giving a portion to Mrs. Blun- dell, and submitting the remainder to the analyst. It was found to be 32 per cent, under proof.—William Frederick Park, a commercial traveller, said that he supplied Mrs. Blundell with the rum complained of. At the time she rece; ved it, fifteen months ago, it was only 22 per cent, under proof, and the difference between then and now must have been caused by evaporations. —Mr. Gillespie said that the bench considered that the rum had not been tampered with, but as it was under proof more than was allowed by law, they must inflict a penalty of 103. and costs. EXTRAORDINARY SCENE AT WAR-

RINGTON.

A scene of a most extraordinary, and for those not directly implicated a most laughable, nature was wit- nessed in Winwick-road and Dickenson-st., Warrington, on Wednesday. It appears, says the "Liverpool Daily Post," that a certain forgesnan had incurred the wrath of the neighbouring women on account of some alleged infelicity. Their wrath reached such a pitch that a score or so waylaid him on Wednesday afternoon on his return to the forge. Besides the women there were as many more children. Together there would be a crowd of some fifty persons. Some were armed with sticks, others with old tin cans, and one with a large tea tray. ; The object of their ire had apparently some hint of what was in store for him,for he took an unusual route to get to the forge. It was of no avail. His enemies ran him to earth in Win wick-road. Then the fun began. He ran and they ran, and as they ran they pelted him with floor, mud.and other refuse from the streets. Through Stamford-st, the strange cavalcade hurried past,women heralding the approach by beating tin cans and keeping up a continuous fusilade of banter at the object or their raillery, on which they likewise landed several hard knocks with tin cans and tea trays. This was kept up till the forge was reaclecl, and the man escaped from further molestation. Then the crowd turned its atten- tion to a di.dy who was also the object of their wrath, and marched in a compact body to Dickenson-street. Apparently they were not expected, as the leaders easily got into the house,the clamorous crowd remaining expectantly outside. They had not long to wait,for out the lady had to come, however unwillingly. The scenes csused the whole neighbourhood to be astir.

===== 0002601_18950316_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
INFLUENZA.

It is not merely in our own little island that influenza is raging with imperial away, for both in Central and Northern Europe it is filling the hospitals and sickrooms, with the same disregard of social status that it is exhibiting in this Country. The districts along the shores of the Mediterranean, from the Pyrenees eastward, appear to be free of it ; but north of this line it is preva- lent ; even Switzerland, notwithstanding its peculiar geographical position and conformation, being a sufferer from the scourge. The informa- tion regarding the country distrilts of Germany, Austria, and France is not specific, but in the large towns of these countries the epidemic is general. We read of schools being closed, of the business of courts being suspended, and of garrisons being in- valided almost wholesale. From Switzerland, through Germany and Austria, along the shores of the Baltic, and into Russia itself, the epidemic has made its way, and not a single doctor in Europe seems to be able to throw the faintest light upon the causes of its progress. The scientific men can tell us nothing of its origin and dissemination; and the practical men can tell us nothing tor its cure except to go to bed and perspire.

===== 0002601_18950316_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
TEA D RUN KA RDS.

A cry is being raised in America about the evil results ; of intemperance in tea-drinldaz. No longer (sacs the " New York Tribune ") may we speak of tea as the cup whigh cheers but not inebriates. It may, indeed, still cheer. It certainly does inebriate, with most deplorable effect, ranking as an intoxicant a good second to alcohol itself. To some, perhaps, the idea of a temperance crusade against the teapot will appear grotesque. Yet in all seriousnesstbaS very thing is urgently needed. According to statistics recently furnished to the'' Medi- cal News " by Dr. James Wood, of Brooklyn, of all the patients applying for treatment at the chief dispensary of that city, no less than 10 per cent, are tea-drunkards. They are not aware of the fact. No one asks to be cured of what we may eall teaomania. But the symptoms of their cases point unmistakably to over-indulgence in tea and that presumption, on inquiry, is confirmed by their confessions. They suffer fro.n headache, vertigo, in- somnia, palpitation of the heart, mental confusion, nightmare, nausea, hallucinations, morbid depression of spirits, and sometimes from suicidal impulses—surely a formidable list of symptoms. These patients are of both sexes and all ages, and confess drinking from a pint and a half to 15 pints of tea each day. Another interesting fact is that nearly one-third of them are of Irish birth. and it is safe to assume that of the nearly two-thirds of Amerioan birth a large proportion are of Irish parent- age. For in Ireland itself tea-poisoning has long been recognised as a widely prevalent evil contributing largely to the number of inmates of insane asylums ; and here, as most housekeepers know, the most inveterate and inordinate tea drinkers are the domestic servants of Irish origin. The evil of tea drink- ing is due, however, not only to the amount consumed, but also to the manner in which it is prepared. An unmeasured quantity of the leaves, says Dr. Wood, is thrown into the teapot, aria an unmeasured quantity of boiling water ad Jed. In any time from ten to 30 minutes this infusion is used. Then new leaves are thrown in with the old, which have been left to soak, aod more water is added, and so on. Sometimes leaves are thus kept soaking for a day or more. The result is , that the decoction is loaded, not only with them, but , with from 7to 17 per cent. ot tannin, and with other even more deleterious substances. This form of pre- paration is almost universal among kitchen servants, and among shop and factory girls, who also are great tea drinkers, and is too often practised among other people of small means, who do not wish to waste a single leaf so long as there is any "strength" in it. There is serious reason to believe that many cares of suicide and insanity are directly duo to tea poisoning, While the number of chronic invalids from the saroe cause in this city alone is to be reckoned .by thousands. It is high time for the evil te be recognised and checked.

HUG BY.

WARRINGTON V. WIGAN. The meeting of these two well-known Lancashire teams always arouses great interest in the towns concerned, as they have been keen rivals for a great number of years. Wigan have up t,) the present had a rather better record to boast of in their struggles with Warrington, but the games are always noted for the keen manner in which they are, fought. The first meeting of the clubs this season took place at Wigan, before the misdeeds of , Wigan were found out and before the club had to do 'time." Wigan were at that time confident that they would be the Lancashire champions,anci great was their chagin when they only made a draw of it with Warring- ton, this being their first check in the competition. How- ever Wigan's interest in the competition has greatly de- creased since then, but nevertheless a large number of their supporters came to Warrington on Saturday. The " gate" was one of the best of the season at Wilderspool as there were over 5,000 present. Mr. Williamson of Salford acted as referee, and the following is the list

of the players : Warrington : Boscow, full back; Barber, Burton, Heesom, and Massey, three quarters ; Bate and Hough- ton, half backs ; Turner Jolley, Dakin, Taylor, Berry, Callaghan, Thorniley, and Bailey ; forwards Wigan Winstanley,full backs,Flowers,Jago,Walkden, and Roberts, three quarters ; Halliwell, and Mitchinson half backs ; Webster, Macnamara, Ainsworth, Rigby Prescott, Benson, Brown, and Derbyshire. Halliwell won the toss, and during the first half War- rington had to face a strong wind. Turner kicked off, and Jago securing, and dodging the Warrington inen,he almost passed Bascow the first minute. The full back however,just grassed him in time. From the subsequent scrimmage, Houghton, who it will be noticed played half back for Warrington for the first time, passed smartly to Barber, who, when looking dangerous was knocked into touch. Warrington further gained ground

by means of Bate and Massey, but the advantage was lost by offside play, and Flowers taking the free kick placed his side on the offensive, and Boscow had an anxious time of it. Several scrimmages took place on the home line, and from one Bate passed to Heesom. The latter's kick was charged down by Webster just on the line, hut Heesom and Burton just staved off the danger. The Warrington forwards then woke up considerably, and forced the Wiganers to beat a retreat. Another free kick was granted to them, but all the advantage was lost by Bailey being tackled off side, and play raged in midfield. Roberts mulled a return from Boscow miser- ably, and Thorniley, Dakin, and Taylor being to hand. they gained considerable ground, which was further supplemented by Burton, Jolley. and Turner, the latter's course being brought to an end by Mitchinson. The Wiganers again returned to the attack, and from a scrimmage under the posts,Halliwell passed to Walkden who unexpectedly dropped a neat goal, amid the loud plaudits of the Wigan section of the spectators. War- rington now played with renewed vigour, and despite the wind, they attacked strongly, Barber and Burton especially distinguishing themselves. The onslaught however, ended in nothing tangible, and the play fluctuated to the other end. Winstaaley made his mark in a very dangerous position, but did not take advantage cf it, kicking across the field, and placing his: forwards "on side," there was every prospect of another score being made, when Boscow stepped in and cleared his lines. Burton Low secured the ball, and by a fine run, took play to half-way, his final pass to Barber going wide. The play at this stage was of the most exciting char- acter, neither side having much advantage. Massey had now to lea re the field temporarily, owing to a slight injury, and Callaghan took his place at threequarter. He was soon tested, and repelled the Wiganers' attack in fine style. Wigan kept up pressure until half time, when they led by a dropped goal to nil. The game was restarted by Webster and Berry mulling the ball, Roberts was almost in, the first minute. The wind had now moderated considerably. Boscow having prevented disaster in the initial portion of the game, Barber, securei the ball and made a grand run to centre, where Fowers got possession. Burton, however refused to let him pat with it, and much amusement was caused by s race between the two in an oblique line across the field, Fowers in the direction of his goal line. Burton finally ran him into touch at the 25 flag. Bate I broke away ir an the line out, and with an almost clear field he preferred to pass to Massey who was promptly collared by Walkden and a capital chalice was lost. Houghton secured from the ensuing scrimmage, and kicking too far, a minor was conceded by Wigan. Turner's reply to the kick out again went over the line and another useless minor was conceded. Wigan now had a spell of attacking, but it was only temporary, and Boscow,Barber,Burton and Heesom sent them to the right-about, and up to the end of the game, Warrington were almost continually attacking. Scrim- mage after scrimmage took place on the line, but War- rington could score nothing but minors and hard lines. The rain now commenced to cotne down in torrents, and the last quarter of an hour's play was completely spoiled , by this means. The ground resembled a puddle,and the players could scarcely get a foodhold. The ball also became sodden. Good play was consequently out of the ' question, and although Warrington pinned their oppon- ents on the line, no score resulted. Heesom near the end , had a capital chance of dropping a goal or scoring a try for the ball was passed to him directly in front of the goal posts with no one to stop him. He tried the drop, and aloud shout arose when the ball appeared to have gone over the bar. The exultation came too soon, how- ever, for the ball sailed on one side, and only a minor came of it. This was Warrington's final chance of equal- ising, and soon afterwards the whistle blew, thus bring- ing., the game to an end with Wigan still leading by a dropped goal to nil. The game, until the rain earns on, was a most enjoyable one, from a spectator's point of view, as the play was of a very open character and rough play being conspicuous by its absence. WARRINGTON ST. WARRINGTON v. ROCHDALE HORNETS. The return match between these two clubs was played at Rochdale on Tuesday. The match was originally fixed for January 26th, out had to be postponed on account of the frost. The weather on Tuesday was very dull, but fortunately 30 rain fell during the afternoon, as in that case, the Hornets ground would have been totally unfit to play upon. As it was, the blades of grass were very few and far between, and the field resembled a quagmire more than a tootball ground. However, the Warringtonians who journeyed to Rochdale were assured that it was splendid compared with the previous week. , If that be the case, the players must have indulged in mudlarking rather than football. There was not a very large number of spectators present—scarcely I,ooo—and with the exception o'f one or two youths, they were not very demonstrative. RUNCORN AGAIN BEATEN BY HALIFAX.

Halifax, the Yorkshire cup holders visited the Irwell- lane enclosure, Runcorn, to play their return engage- ment on Saturday. Additional interest was centred in the match inasmuch as the home team have not yet been able to beat their opponents. When the two teams filed into the enclosure, it was found that they were composed as follows: Runcorn :—Houghton, full back; Gregory, Warder, West, and %V. Gayter, threequarter- backs ; Abrams and J. Faulkner, half backs; W. Faulk- ner, J. Langley, H. Farmer, F. Gayter, Lightfoot, Williams, Johnson and S. Walker, forwards. Halifax: —A. Chorley, full back; Firth, W. Chorley. Bottotnley, and 'Foothill, threequarter-backs ; Rigg and Arnold, half backs; Mellor, Wilson, Ripley, Dickenson, Knowles, Webster, Robertshaw, and Kitson, forwards. Houghton was successful in winning the toss, and elected to defend the east goal, which gave the Runcorn team the advan- tage of the wind. Webster commenced operations, Gregory making a good return, and quick following up by the home forwards enabled F. Gayter to charge Bot- tomley'a kick, causing the first pack to be formed in the Tykes' 25. Halifax were penalized, and Houghton took a free kick, but it came to naught, and Firth removed the danger to midfield. Beautiful passing between Abrams, J. Faulkner, Warder, West, and W. Gayter fol- lowed, the latter being held close to the line. On the ball becoming loose, it was kicked down the line, and an exciting race followed, Gregory smartly gathering the oval and rushing over. Warder took the place, but no goal accrued. From the drop out, more passing by the Runcornians followed, W. Faulkner, Warder, and Gre- gory participating in a movement which almost ended in the latter getting through, A. Chorley effecting a good tackle. They experienced the hardest of luck on two occasions. Exciting play now followed both teams play- ing; grandly. Firth got in several tricky runs,which caused the Runcornians to be pressed back until Abrams dribbled finely away from a scrimmage, and the venue was changed to the centre. Houghton was next en evid- ence with a judicious kick, and the visiting custodian was upset near his own line, through the quick follow- ing up of the borne forwards,and once more the visitors' citadel was subjected to a strong attack. The fine de- fensive play of Rigg, however, prevented disaster more than once. A free kick was granted to Runcorn for offside play by Arnold, and Warder, who took the shot, had the hardest of luck, the ball striking the uprights and rebounding the wrong side of the posts. Rigg raised the siege by snapping a Runcorn pass and got clear away, and a round of passing followed which seriously threatened the borne defence until W. Gayter stepped into the breach. The interval arrived with the score : Runcorn, one try ; Halifax, nil. W. Faulkner re-started and the opening was almost sensational, as Farmer rac- ing up, got possession, and passed to W. Gayter, who was only held just outside by Firth. Gradually Halifax eased the pressure, and fine kicking by their backs caused Houghton to be troubled. There was now little to choose between either team, and the game was of a ding-dong character, end to end visits following each other. After a lot of kicking, the ball was sent item the home line, and Johnson touched down but the referee ordered a five yards scrimmage and a quick pass from Rigg to Toothill enabled that player to drop a goal. From the restart the game underwent a great change, the Tykes who are old cup fighters, and having once got the lead, evidently from their tactics, meant to keep it, and they wasted a lot of time by kicking the ball out of play and keening it as tight as possible in the packs. As the game was approaching the close Runcorn made desper- ate efforts to rub off the odd point, and Abrams and W. Gayter were within an ace of scoring, the former mak- ing some grand attempts,whilst the for wards worked like Trojans, and at times fairly rushed the burly Yorkshire- men in some of the scrimmages, but it was all to no r ur- pose. ENID ax defended as desperately as Runcorn attacked. The game ended in a win for Halifax by one dropped goal (four points), to Runcorn, one try (three points).

ASSOCIATION.

EARLESTOWN V. NORTH MEOLS LANCASHIRE ALLIANCE. —North Meals visited Earlestown to play off their first fixture in the Lancashire Alliance on Saturday after- noon. There was a very fair attendance of spectators. The visitors kicked ,Off down hill, and for a short time held a slight advantage, but Earlestown broke away and made a smart attack on the Meols goal. The defence, however, was 'pod, ..nd they were sent to the right about. Come loose play ensued which gave the visito.s an opening, but they l'Ailer i”.aprve on the advan- tage. Again Earleqown took up the attack, and some good passing in front of goal resulted in Alfred Clarke , notching the first point for the home team. Earlestown , now did all the pressing, and before half time was reached had added two more goals. On turning round the game resolved itself into a bombardment of the visitors' goal, but owing to the series of mistakes the Meals forwards were let in, and they succeeded in regis- tering their first and only goal. They, however, after- wards never got beyond the half way line, and the game ended in a win for Earlestown by 9 goals to 1.

THE "OLD HOUSE IT HOME."

PROPOSED RE-BUILDING.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Thursday morning, before the Mayor (chairman), and Messrs. W. Pierpoint, J. Burgess, T. Birchall, J. H. Fleming, Col. Ridgway, and H. Roberts, NIL R. Davies, solicitor, appeared on behalf of Messrs Greenall, Whitley and Co., and applied for the sanctioning of plans for the re-build- ing of the' Old House at Home," Friar's Green, which, he said, had been an old house ever since he could re- memember, and a public-house for about two or three years. It was a question of taking advantage of this opportunity of widening the turning from Friar's Gate into Friar's Green, and negotiations had proceeded for the purpose.of arranging if possible some plan for im- proving the corner. In the end Messrs. Greenall, Whit- ley and Co., who were the owners of the house, agreed for the purchase of the corner which was once covered with trees. The object had been instead of re-building the house upon the old site. Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co.,'would, to improve the corner, give a portion of this land and a portion of the land already licensed to. form part of the site. The point was, to give the street at the career 8 feet additional width. He said tho only building which would be effected by any possible nuis- ance in the matter was the Y.M.C.A., and Mr. Owen (the architect), had taken particular care that the liv- ing port of the house should be at the end where that Institution stood,and the business part of it at the other end, and he had taken great care not to interfere with any light that would come across the street, by making the new premises one storey high, so as to make as much light as possible in the warner and it was that building past which it was the in- tention of the Corporation to drive a new street across the old Bowling Green into Rylands-et. The Town Clerk : It is suggested. Mr. Davies : This would afford an opportunity by leaving plenty of room. The area is increased which the house would cover, but that was owing to the fact that part of the premises would only. be one storey high. He did not think any question would arise as to the fee'; -Is which the magistrates might have upon beerhouses. The Licensing Committee had no power over the house as it could be built on the present land without any interference from anyone. Mr. Owen would explain that there was give and take in the matter on each side. so as to effect the improvement, and he thought their Worships would see that every effort had been made to study the convenience of the eeig,rhbour- hood, and also to meet the wishes of the town. Mr. Davies then pointed out the different objects ma the plan and said the portion to be taken ever would increase the width of the street to 28 feet.and make the corner very convenient. Mr. Fleming : Is that extension to adjoin the house in Friars'-gate ? Mr. Owen: Yes, to come up to that point. The Town Clerk said the Corporation had agreed to purchase the land from Messrs. Greenall, Whitley, and Co., and it was suggested that they have a street frcm Rylands-st. to that point. Mr. Pierpoint said he understood that the land to be utilised as a street improvement was to be bought by the Corporation, and that Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co. were giving nothing. The Town Clerk : Nothing. The Mayor would like to know what improvement upon the street would be effected. The Town Clerk It would widen the street Bft. Mr. Burgess: What does it taper down to. Mr. Owen: Five feet at one end and nothing at the other. The actual area thrown into the street is 33 square yards. Mr. Davies, in answer to Mr. Pierpoint, said the ques- tion had occurred to him that he must not represent as giving anything to the Corporation but at the same time the Company had had to pay out of their pockets what they would not have had to do if it had not been for allowing the Corporation that land, as part of it be- longed to the licensed premises. Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co. were doing as much as they could and they wished the bench to understand that. Mr. Owen said the Corporation had fixed the 281 t. and the; had to work to it. It was stated that the old street was 21ft.wide and the new one would be 28ft. wide. The Bench then retired in consultation with the Magistrates' Clerk (Mr. H. B. White). Mr. Owen and Mr. Wynne Jones, and on their return the Mayor said that the decision was adjourned for a fortnight to give Mr. Wynne Jones time to confer with his colleagues.

EXTENSION OF THE B0801:761i.

The CLERK read the folk 'in letter dated March 11 from the Local Government Board with reference to the proposed extension of the borough of Warrington: "Sir, —I am directed by the Local Government Board to state that they have under their consideration the represen- tation made by their inspector, Colonel Ducat, after his recent visit to Warrington, for the purpose of ascertain- ing and reporting to the Board the facts requisite to enable them to decide as to whether the representation of the Town Council in favour of the alteration of the boundary of the borough is one in regard to which a formal inquiry should be held, the Board have decided to direct a formal inquiry, to be held in the matter, and such inquiry will be opened at the Town Hall, Warring- ton, on Tuesday, April 2, at 10.30 a.m. I am to add that in making any arrangements that may be consi- dered desirable for the representation of their interests at the inquiry, the Warrington District Highway Board must understand that if it should be found imprac- ticable to conclude the inquiry in one day, it will be continued de die in diem, until its completion, and that the hours of the different sittings will be regulated by the inspector. C. A. CALTON."—It was decided to refer the letter to the Committee appointed to watch the Board's interest in the matter.

A. COMPLAINT FROM WINWICK. A letter was read from the Winwick Parish Council, calling the Board's attention to the state of Alder-lane, Winwick, and the footpath over Hall's fields, Orford, which were in a very bad condition.—The Board de- cided to repair the footpath and lane, subject to obtain- ing permission of the land owners.

DEATH. OF DR. R. W. DALE

A USEFUL CAREER.

Dr. R. W. Dale, the eminent independent minister of Birmingham, died suddenly at his residence at about six on Wednesday evening. He had been ia a precarious condition for some days, suffering from complications affecting the kidneys and heart. Early yesterday a more favourable repert was made concerning him, and he appeared to have gained strength, promising a possible recovery for a short time. He died, however, quite suddenly from syncope. Dr. Dale (says " Men and Women of the Time ") was chairman of the Congregational Union of England and 1 Wales, 1868.69. For seven years he edited the " Con- gregationalist," and he is the author of " Discourses on Special Occasions," " Week-day Sermons," a " Life of the Rev. J. A. James," " Discourses on the Epistles to the Hebrews," "The Ten Commandments,' "The Ultimate Principle of Protestantism," "The Atonement : a series of lectures prepared at the request of the Con- gregational Union of England and Wales," which has been translated into French and German; "The Evangelical Revival," " Lectures on the Epistle to the Ephesians," , " A Manual of Congregational Principles," Laws of Christ for Common Liftl," " Impressions of Australia," and articles in the " British Quarterly, Nineteenth Cen- tury, Fortnightly, and Contemporary Review." He also edited a translation of " Reus on the Theology of the Apostolic Age." In 1877 he delivered, at Yale College, Connecticut, a series of lectures on " Preaching," being the first Englishman appointed to the Lyman Beecher Lectureship,and in the same year he receive' from Yale the degree of D.D. The lectures have since been pub- lished both in England and America. In 1883 he received from Glasgow the degree of L.L.D. He was formerly vice•chairman of the Birmingham School Board, and has been app9inted by the Senate of the University of London, governor of King-Ed ward Vl.'s School, Bir- mingham. In 1888 he was appointed a member of the Royal Commission on the Elementary Education Acts, and he signed the minority report. Dr. Dale took an active part in tho Nonconformist controversies and poli- tical movements. In the years following the introduc- tion of Mr. Gladstone's first Home Rule Bill, in 1886, he gave powerful support to the Unionist cause, and 1888 severed his connection with the Birmingham Liberal I Association.

plan of sending a "recruiting ship" round the coast has been very successful, but as the recruits in this case are boys it will need a conaiderably longer period to make them efficient seamen.

IF we may judge from the cor- The Ship Canal. respondence in the Manchester

papers the Ship Canal Company are, in a financial sense, rapidly getting into a tight place. The correspondents are for the most part well-wishers of the Company, and they make ' numerous suggestions for its benefit. The only drawback to most of their schemes is that they are not practicable. By no method of financing does it seem possible out of funds in hand or that will be in hand when the time comes to provide for interest on the debenture stock. It is true that traffic is increasing at what some people re- gard as a satisfactory rate, but while that may be some consolation for those who can afford to wait, it will. do little towards satisfying the more immediate and urgent claims. The more closely do people scan the situation the more do they seem to be driven to the conclusion that the Manchester Corporation must again act the part of the good fairy. In the meantime it is ominously pointed out that by clause 13 of the Ship Canal Act, if there are arrears of either debenture interest or capital, or both, to the extent of £lO,OOO, a receiver may be appointed by the mortgagees. The troubles of the Canal Company are not confined to financial matters. The Company is, says one correspon- dent, hemmed in by a perfect network of diffi- culties in regard to maintaining a certain depth in the canal,"and in this connection the clauses cf the Act relating to Warrington are particularly quoted. If it fails to carry out the obligations it has entered into it will make itself liable to untold penaltios in one way or another, and it is perti- nently asked, where is the money to come from either to carry out the obligations or to pay the penalties in default. The same correspondent continues, "In the interest of the shareholders these dangers and difficulties must be faced. It is idle to live in a fool's paradise rejoicing over the increase of traffic, when any receipts there may be are in danger of going into the hands of a receiver or of being paid away in litigation and penalties. In the event of unsuccessful litigation and no funds, what will become of the property of the Company ?" The directors will evidently need all the business abilities and financial resources at their command to turn the awkward corner which is in front of them.

MR. BRYCE is the President of the Trade. Board of Trade and has excep

tional sources of information on commercial matters. The remarks which he made at a electing of members of Chambers of Commerce in London on Wednesday as to a probable speedy revival of trade are, therefore, of more than usual interest. Since 1846, he said, the periods of trade- depression had been in no instance longer than six years, and as the .present depression had lasted for about five years there would now prob- ably be a change for the better. He believed there were already visible signs of the much-desired reviv- al. The movements of finance had been distinctly brisker within the last few weeks,and although the trade returns for February were less than in the same month last year that was probably due to the exceptionally hard weather. The settlement of the American tariff question had also had a dis- tinctly beneficial effect, as the trade with the United States was distinctly reviving. On the whole, therefore, according to the President of the Board of Trade, the prospects of trade are encouraging and are rather light than dark.

Very few people can have missed noticing the obtrusive methods of cheap advertising adopted by the I.L.P. or its supporters. On almost every wall, fence, or boarding is to be seen scrawled in chalk the announcement that such and such per- son will speak somewhere, coupled with a cordial invitation to come and be instructed, or a stern in- junction to read such and such a paper. Turn where you will these very informal announce- ments stare you in the face. If there is any re- muneration attached to such a plan someone is making a "pile," but perhaps the I.L.P. men have becoms so eager to win support that each carries a piece of white chalk wherewith to make public his sentiments. Prom an advertising point of view the idea is not bad, but what say the pro- perty owners whose erections are so lavishly " ornamented" free of charge. Even a burglary, it seems, is not always an un- mixed evil. At least we think that this will be the opinion of Mr. B. B. Matthews, the model and genial chairman of the Little Bankey Parish Coun- cil. It will be remembered that the house at which Mr. Matthews resides was broken into a few weeks ago by a couple of men who were, however, alarmed in the midst of tneir operations, and hastily departee, carrying with them some port- able property. It now appears from " Pearson's. Weekly " that Mr. Matthews has put in a claim on the burglary insurance scheme of that paper, and has been awareled .£lO. At this rate some people would almost be disposed to describe a burglary as a " blessing in disguise."

' The subject of the borough extension has fre- quently been alluded to in these columns. Last week we expressed our pleasure that the townships of 1 Little Sankey and Latchford Without had consent- ed to be absorbed within the borough. The two other townships—Poulton-with-Fearnhead and Appleton—which are affected, have hitherto been strongly opposed to the:proposed extension in their direction, but it is believed that even here the op- position is becoming weaker, owing to the advan- tageous terms offered by the Corporation. It is probable that the strip of Poulton required, will be obtained without much difficulty, and in Appleton, it is stated that some of the largest ratepayers affected are agreeable to coming within the borough. Taking everything into consideration, all the omens are favourable for Warrington, and the good old t:own will probably be considerably larger in area in a few:months' time.

Since November, when the present Board of Guardians was elected, the fortnightly meetings have been much more protracted than they usually were previously. Meetings of half an hour were very frequent,and it was something very importan indeed which kept the Board together an hour. Of late, however, the meetings have averaged an hour or more, and therefore when on Wednesday the bu,iness was completed in twenty-five minutes, everyone was astonished. The members had given their tongues a rest, which is very commendable now and again.

It is astonishing to notice the hold, the P.S.A.'s and otner Sunday,afternoon meetings have obtained upon the Warrington people. Two years ago, the first P.,S.A. meeting was el d in Wycliffe Church, and at that time no one expected them to reach their present dimensions. In fact many people pro- phesied failure. instead of failure, however, they have been a brilliant success. THE Navy Estimates which were I The Navy. submitted to Parliament on Tuesday would have made the old economists "gasp and stare." That £18,000,000 should be spent upon the Navy in one year would have seemed incredible to them. Yet that is the amount of expenditure which is pro- vided for the coming year, and it is apart altoge- ther from a loan of £9,000,000 which is to be raised for special purposes of naval defence. The growth of the Navy estimates has, in recent years, been extremely rapid. Ten years ago they were £12,000,000, now they are, as we have said, .£18,000,000, a growth of exaCtly fifty per cent. Ten years the number of men asked for to man the fleet was 58,000, now it is 88,850. Ten years ago the tonnage of the fleet was 692,000, now it is 1,300,000. Yet, in spite cf this great and steady increase, it has probably rarely happened in pre- vious years that the Estimates have met with so little adverse criticism as on the present occasion. The Government appear to,lave satisfied not only an overwhelming majority of their own followers, but also the "captious critics" of the Opposition bench. As a matter of fact, it is universally agreed that the one thing which this country can not dispense with in the way of defensive armaments is a strong ifleet, that is to say, a fleet which, in number of ship and weight of metal, would, in case of emer- gency, be equal to the task of defending the coasts of this country and the world-wide commerce and ' possessions of the Empire. And, unfortunately, it does not rest with the British Government to , say finally when the Navy is sufficiently powerful to answer all the purposes for which it is main- tained. The pace is really set by foreign nations. Doubtless ten years ago when the expenditure was one-third less than it is now, the Navy was considered sufficiently powerful to hold its own against any possible foreign assault. But much has happened since then. Some countries, and particularly France, have been making constant and strenuous efforts to strengthen their forces at sea, and there are Frenchmen and French journals who have not hesitated to say that all this was done with a view to a possible conflict with England. Under these circum- stances, it is certain that no English Government, whether it be Liberal or Tory, will think of starv- ing or stinting the Navy. Where the mad race for increased armament will end it is impossible to say. There are people who maintain that the best hopes of peace is to be found in the constant improvement of warlike weapons, as these in the end will be made so destructive that war, and particularly an aggressive war, will become practi- cally impossible. On the same style of reasoning, it might appear that the only limit to the growth of expenditure on preparation for war will be found in the bankruptcy or financial exhaustion of the rival nations. Out of the loan of £9,000,000 which is to be raised some very extensive improvements are to be made at various harbours and naval stations. ' At Portland £700,000 will be spent in extending' the harbour and in making it impenetrable by torpedoes, and a similar sum will be spent at Gib- raltar. Large sums of money will be spent in , making a new harbour at Dover, and amongst the , foreign stations that will receive attention is that ,1 of Hong-Kong which, in view of Eastern compli- cations, might, of course, become of very great importance. One of the greatest difficulties which the Admiralty has to contend with is that of pro- viding sailors for the rapidly augmenting fleet. It appears that the inducements which are held out, are not sufficient to draw sailors away from the inert:wade marine. On the other hand, the,

To emphasize the success which Sunday afternoon meetings have obtained,tt is interesting to mention that besides Wycliffe Church being crowded, there. was at the same time a crowded meeting at the Y.M.C.A., and a very large meeting at Bold-A. Wes- leyan Church.

Some Authorities are extremely slow in moving, but we think that the Mersey and Irwell Joint Committee are a little slower than usual in a notice which they sent to the Warrington Rural District Council, and which was read at their last meet- ing. They gave notice that if the Council or any individual tipped snow into the river they would proceed against them, or him. If that notice had been sent in November or the beginning of -December, there would have been some sense in it, but to send it when the winter is practically over seems to be a waste of paper and ink, and a postage stamp. Perhaps, however, they are intending it for next year, and if that be so they can fairly claim to have" taken time by the forelock."

The estimates for the army and navy for the year 1895-6 are not of a character to fill the hearts of the members of the Peace Society with satisfaction. The estimated expenditure upon the army is £17,973,800—0n1y £22,100 less than last year, while the navy estimate is .C 18,701,000 an increase of .C 1,334,900. Thus on the two services a total amount of ..C36,674,800 is required, which is about one third the total expenditure of this country. No one cam view these figures with anything like satisfaction.. for they show that one third of the taxation of this country is spent, not for its development, but to • prepare for the brutal, inhuman scourge of war. The principles of the Peace Society whizh are after all, but the principles of the religion professed by the nations of Europe, are at a discount at the pre-- sent time, for instead of attempts at disarming the enormous armies of Europe, they are being con— stantly increased, and it is difficult to prophesy what the end will be. A great war seems to be the. only solution to the present situation, for the. bloated armaments are becoming intolerable- burdens upon the nations of Europe.

The speech which Lord Cross made at the Church Defence meeting at Warrington last week was of the "forcible-feeble" order. It contained plenty of strong epithets such as " monstrous lies,"- " fallacies," " utter untruths," " ridiculous proposi— tions," and so on, but in reality it was a weak and illogical deliverance. When, for instance, he declares that there is no such thing as a Welsh Church and that the Church in England and is Wales is "undivided and indivisible "does he mean that the Church in Wales can not be disestablished and disendowed except along with the Church ia England? If that be so be is hugely mistaken,and he will no doubt live to see how easily it can be done. So far as the promoters and supporters of the Welsh Disestablishment Bill are concerned it is. not very material whether the Welsh Church is called the Church of Wales or the Church in Wales. What they are concerned with is that the Welsh people through an overwhelming majority of their representatives in Parliament demand disestablish- ment and disendowment,and that there are other and in their opinion conclusive reasons in favour of this reform. And, again, is it not illogical of Lord Cross to assert in one and the same speech, first, that the Welsh Church is really part and parcel of the Church of England, and, second, to deny that it is an alien' church ? Does Lord Cross deny that there is such a, thing as Welsh natienality ? Everyone knows that church defenders do not stick at arguments which are utterly Inconsistent with the teaching of history,but we did not know that they treated with equal disdain the facts of geography and, ethnology.

Mr. Robert Pierpoint's speech at the same meet- ing was, as usual, of that flowery. character which sounds well but generally means nothing. Ile- " appealed to Nonconformists to unite witn the- Church," and " cause the world to exclaim as of old, 'Behold how these Christians love one another.'" It would be well if Mr. Pierpoint en- deavoured to persuade the Church to love the Non- conformists a little more. Times without number in Warrington have the Nonconformists invited the church to unite with them in various movements, but the church ministers have invariably refused , to co-operate. The Nonconformists are always willing to assist the churchmen in anything to ifl: prove the town, but a more exclusive body of gen- tlemen than the church parsons it would be diffi- cult to find. If all the churches are put on an equal footing in the eyes of the state, there would be much better chance of showing the world ',how , these Christians love ono another."

STOCKTON HEATH.

THE CHURCH HISTORY LECTURE.,---The sixth lecture en English Church History WAS delivered in the Institute on' Monday evening. Mr. T. Jackson Lee presided, and announced tl,e siihject of the lecture to be the "Leading ‘,-nt in ts;,e anon," covering the period AD. 1535-1590. TI •lecur.r (Mr. H. J. Westbrook) said the yuccas to ui cJosidered in the present lecture were—the dissolution of the monasteries ; the translation of holy scripture ; the influence of Swims reformers under Edward VI. and the reaction against them under Mary ; concluding with the Elizabethan settlement. Each of these several points was dealt with in turn, some 55' excellent slides being exhibited by M. H. N. Houghton in illustration. One of the points mostly insisted upea was the continuity of Church organisation during the Tudor period. The dissolution of the monasteries did not mean the destruction of the English Church, for though monasticism from the earliest times had been cart it was not the vital part of the' Church's system. But the diocesan and parochial systems were the Church's very life blood,her arterial and nervous organisation, and as these were never interfered with in the Tudor times, the continuity of Church organisation was indisputable. The ordina- ries retained their jurisdiction, and administered the law as before. The bishops still sat in the House of. Lords, and by the same title as before. The Convoca- tions continued to sit concurrently with every Perlis- rnent as before. Bishops and parochial clergy retained their respective positions, performed their duties in the same churches,to the same congregations. Corruption were cut away, the usurped power of the Popes was successfully overthrown, but no new church was founded." f WALTON PARISH COUNCIL MEETING o .—A meeting - this Council was held on Saturday night. Mr. Monks rt' presided, and there were also present—Messrs. Ca wright, Richardson,Beswick, and Mounfield (clerk). T. 11. deputation appointed to wait upon the Town Clerk las" Thursday reported the result of their interview, It WO decided to accept the terms offered by the Corporationj and not to oppose the proposed extension of the boreag° of Warrington. "LA GRIPPE." Many persons in Runcorn and district are confined to their rooms suffering from influenza, although it is, stated that the malady is of a somewhat milder type than has been the case en previous occasions. Amongst local sufferers may be mentioned the Rev. Canon Mait- land Wood and the Rev. T. Brackenbury, Wesleyan sugeristendent. Beth gentlemen are said to be improving. A BIG CARGO AT RUNCORN. The barque Glitner with bone, ash from River Plate drawing 16it. 41n., arrived at the Runcorn docks on Monday with over 600 tons on board, and is considered to be the largest vessel that has come up toe tidal way to Runcorn tor many years. The tide was over 201 d. at Liverpool and about 17tt. at Runcorn. BRUNSWICK MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT SOCIETY. At the meeting of this Society on Wednesday evening Mr. J. Foyer read an interesting paper on "Advertis- ing." From a business point of view he strongly advo- cated advertising. A discussion afterwards ensued in which Messrs. W. Massey, J. D. Jones, W. Millington, and J. Smith took part. The chair was occupied by Mr. T. Simister.

COLLISION ON THE SHIP CANAL AT RUNCORN. A serious collision occurred on Wednesday during a fog near Runcorn Bridge. The steamer Jenny was tied up in the Ship Canal, when another steamer forcibly collided with her, thus causing considerable damage. She had her timbers staved in, and the cabin was par- tially shattered. The Jenny is undergoing repairs at Runcorn.

DEATH OF AN OLD INHABITANT. Mrs. Alice Handley, 28, Church-st., Runcorn, died on Wednesday afternoon, after a prolonged illness, at the advanced age of 88 years. Her husband, who was a pork butcher, pre-deceased her 30 years ago, and the business has since been carried on by her at the address stated above, she having been at the shop for almost half a century. She retained her faculties up to the last,with the esception of defective sight. She was very well known in the town.

SEAMEN'S INSTITUTE, RUNCORN. An entertainment at this Institute on Tuesday even- ing was provideu by the Moseley Hill Temperance choir. The programme consisted of songs, piano solos, recita- tions, duets and choruses, which were creditably given. The Rev. J. H. Dawson, who had been instrumental in bringing the choir to Runcorn, contributed a reading and a song. Duets on auto-harp and violin were given by Messrs. Hartley and Wilson, and other contributors were Miss Wilson, Miss Chadwick, Mr. A. Orton Mr. Goulder, and Mr. Chadwick. A vote of thanks was tendered to the Rev. J. H. Dawson,and the choir, on the proposition of Mr. Shaw, seconded by Mr. W. Byfield. ST. JOHN'S AMBULANCE ASSOCIATION.—RUNCORN BlieNcw.

Ridgway, John M. 80-iworth, William Williams. Mr. David Smith passed the final examination, and is entitled to a medal. TEE DISTRESS IN RUNCORN.

Fewer applications are now being made to the local ward committees for relief than has been the ease tor some time past. The subscriptions to the fund, too, have fallen off considerably, the only additional sub- scription tube mentioned being one of £lO trom Messrs. lireenall, Whitley and Co., Limited. Free breakfasts to children are still being continued at the schools. The employees of the Runcorn Co-operative Society have during the distress, provided 6,000 people with dinners of soup and bread, and have distributed at Weston and Weston Point 100 four lb. loaves. They also intend to give 50 four lb. leaves to be distributed to those in dis- tress at Weston and Weston Point during the next three weeks. The employees have also contri- buted to the relief fund in the town the SUM of £4 103., and also £1 to Weston relief fund. RUNCORN BURIAL BOARD.

A meeting of this Beard was held at the cemetery offices en Wednesday afternoon, when Mr. E. Imisen, JP., occupied the chair. "rhere were also present : Dr. Gastaldi, Mr. S. Taylor, J. P., C.C., Mr. R. Mmis, and Mr. Salkeld (secretAry). The fees rece;ved for the eleven monzhs to February 25th, were £263 9s. 6d. as compared with £451 is. 2,1. decrease £lB7 lie. Bd. TONIC FOL-FA CONCRRT AT RUNCORN. , There was not a very large attendance at the concert given by the Runcorn Tonic Sol-fa prize choir in the Public Hall, Runcorn, on Wednesday evening, and this , was a matter of regret as the proceeds were in aid of the distress fund. The concert nevertheless was of a high 'order, and Mr. Holford, the conductor of the choir , is to be congratulated upon the successful performance of the choir. The glees, part songs, and choruses were listened to with unalloyed pleasure, and the renderation of them was an exemplification of what is possible by the Tonic Sol-fa s“itern. The singing of the two part songs "The lullaby of life" (H. Leslie) and" Lochnagar" (J. Bell),are woi thy of special mention,this latter Scotch song, which was given by desire, being a capital finish to the concert. The soloists were Miss Cross (Winsfcrd), Mr. Berry and Mr. C. Leathwood, and all sang in such good style as to receive hearty receptions. Miss Cross's two songs were " Angels ever bright and fair" and " Watching Alone," and she gave an excellent rendition of both. Mr. Berry contributed a couple of recitatives and airs from Handel's " Messiah "and " Tephtha " to the first part of the programme, and his singing was in every way a succeqs. In the second part he sang " A dream of Spain" for which be was deservedly encored. Mr. C. Leathwood rendered the recitative "I feel the Daity within" and the air "Arts, arm ye brave" (Judas Maccabceus) which were well suited to his style, but he was not as successful in the selection of his last song "Molly Carew." Mr. William Cooke was the accom- panist. THE HALEWOOD RECTOR AND THE VILLAGE POSTMASTER.

LIBEL ACTIONS SETTLED.

On Thursday, at the Liverpool Assizes, before Mr. Justice Lawrence and a spncial jury, the libel action of Smith v. Wareing come on for hearing. The plaintiff was the Rev. H. Gibson Smith, M.A., rector of Halewood, near Widnes, and the defendant was Jona Wareing, postmaster and grocer at Halewood. Mr. Bigham, Q.C. and Mr. M'Conkey were equnsel for the plaintiff, and defendant was represented by Mr. Pickford, Q.C. and Mr. A. G. Steel. Mr. Bigham stated that the libel complained of was contained in a leaflet which came into exist- ence about the time that the elections for the parish and district councils were taking place. Mr. Wareing interested himself a good deal in local poll- -1 tics,but took a different view from that of the Rector. Some time ago Mr. R. W. Brade was the master of the Church schools at Halewood, and his engagement was terminated, not for any misconduct or blame attaching to him, by the rector and Mr. R. B. Neilson, who were on the committee of management, the letter being writ- ten by the rector. On the 14th November last a hotpest supper was held at flalewood,and at that gathering Mr. Mather, solicitor, of Liverpool, made a speech in which he introduced some jocular observations as to the amicable way in which Halewood had been able to get rid of its Radical schoolmaster. The plaintiff was at that gathering, and if he had thought a slur had been intended upon Mr. Brade he would have put matters right, but he treated the matter simply as a joke. The speech however,was reported in a local paper, and a ecpv of it was sent by Mr. Wareing to Mr. Brade, who wrote the libel complained of, and sent it to two newspapers, only ome of which, however, published it. With that, of course, Mr. Wareing had nothing to do, but it had occurred to some people that it would be a good thing if the letter were printed in the form of a leaflet and distributed amongst the voters in the Progressive as against toe Moderate interest. A largo number of the leaf- lets were printed and sent to a ratepayers' meeting at Halewood. It was, however, resolved not to distribute them, but Mr. Wareing appeared to have taken some of them, sending a copy to Mr. Brade and another to a Mr. Thomas Rogers. There was no doubt according to law that defendant published the libel, and he thoeght the only question to be decided by the jury was as to what were proper damages to be awarded to the rector. Mr. Bighorn then read the lea&t, which commenced be referring to Mr. Mather's speech at the hotpot supper. Smith and Neilson (school managers fe-r- -%oath) who will sit down to gloat over applause for actions which deprive a man of his living, of his rights, of freedom? Ought electors to continue to place the trust of true education to such men, whose God is Toryism and Terrorism." Correopondence took place between the rector's solicitor and Mr. Wareinz's solicitor,but it was not until the 23rd January that Mr. Wareing's lawyer wrote saying that his client was prepared to offer the rector and Mr. Neilson a full and ample apology and retractation,and to pay the expenses of the action as be- tween solicitor and client. Ultimately the parties came to a settlement, after withdrawing and apologising. . Three of the libel actions sf a Eimilar nelture were also I amicably settled.

THE BOILER EXPLOSION AT ATHERTON'S QUAY.

ACTION FOR £234 DAMAGES AT THE COUNTY

COURT.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Wynne Floulkes, Peter Lowe, 2, Leinster-street, Runcorn, sued Richard Abel, flat owner, Runcorn, for £234, as compensation under the Employers Liability Bill, for the loss of his son by a boiler explosion on the steam flat "Amy," belonging to Mr. Abel, which took place in the River Mersey near Atherton's Quay,on September lA, 1894, the explosion being alleged to be owing to the negligence of Mr. Abel in employing a man named Senior, who was alleged to be incompetent, to examine the boiler. _ _ .

... .—..„ Mr. Jordan, barrister, Manchester, represented the plahatiff, and Mr. Collingwood Hope, barrister, Liver- pool, instructed by Mr. Fowler, of Messrs. Davies, Kirk- council, and Burgess represented the defendant. • Mr. Jordan in opening the case for the plaintiff said that the action was to recover damages under the Em- ployers' Liability Bill, by Peter Lowe, father of the yourig. man named Ralph Lowe, 23 years of age, who met his death on September 18, 1824, from an explosion of a boiler on a steam barge, called the "Amy," which was lying in the Mersey near Warrington. Ralph Lowe at the time of the explosion was engaged as engine man upon the barge by a man named Gibbons who was cap- tain of the barge, and employed by the defendant. It appeared from a conversation which took place with Mr. Hough, the cashier of Mr. Abel, that Gibbons was instructed by Mr. Abel to employ a man to take the post which resulted in Lowe's death. He only survived a short time after the explosion took place, as he was so severely injured. The negligence alleged to have taken place on the part of Mr. Abel, the defendant, was the employing of a man named Senior to make an examine- of the boiler in question, as Senior was a man not really qualified or capable of making such examination whatever. A Board of Trade inquiry had been made in Runcorn on the question and had brought out much information upon the subject. The boiler had had a varied experience, having been sunk in the river, and left for some time, and it had been repaired some three and a half years ago. He then employed Senior to look at the boiLr to report upon its coadition,, and report upon the repairs which were required to be made. Tne examination, according to the Board of Trade had been quite ineffectual, as he had not examined a patch in the combustion charnber,behind which the stay had corroded very much. The pressure accustomed to be used in tho boiler was 801tre., whereas the evidence of Mr. Shott. Board of Trade engineer, showed that it was not fit to bear more than 10lbs. Senior had given directions for slight repairs, and after these had been done, Mr. Abel used the boiler again. L'e (Mr. Jordon) contended that Mr. Abel ought to have made more inquiries as to the fitness of Mr. Senior to make the examination. Mr. Abel in hisstatement before the inquiry,said that he had no experience of boilers and, therefore, he had left the matter in the hands of Mr. Senior, but he had evidence which showed that Mr. Abel had owned a steam fiat pre- viously, and., therefore, had some slight knowledge as to what was required. The fact of the boiler having exploded owing to being 'defective was presumptive negligence which the defence would have to meet and disprove. George Shott, engineer and surveyor to the Board of Trade, eaid that he was appointed to make a prelimi- nary inquiry for the Beard of Trade into the cause of the explosion on September 19th. When he made his examination he found that the combustion chamber had given way at the central stay, and the adjacent stays had also given way, and the whole of the fire box was forceJ upwards, and the tubes were forced through the front tube plate, fracturing the plate through the weak- it line of tubes. The central ,tay had corroded vert ranch at the rivetted head, and had been covered LI a plate patch. The plate around the stay was also corroded to , a great extent. The explosion was caused through the ' central stay being corroded at its head, and also the plate around it being corroded, which caused the plate to give way at that part, the plate not being able to withstand the pressure which was in the boiler,and which was 501bs, at the time of the explosion. He should say that the boiler was not fit for any useful pressure at the time of the explosion. The corrosion could only be de- tected by removing the patch. If a person had been employed to examine the boiler thoroughly, the patch would have been removed. No one cauld have examined the boiler thoroughly without removing the patch. The patch did not appear to have been removed when it had been previously examined. There was nothing else which could have caused the explosion. By Mr. Hope : He was present at the Board of Trade inquiry. He did not remember anyone say that the boiler was made of exceptionally good iron. The tubes appeared to be new. He made no examination of the engine. One of the stays appeared to have been recently filled in. The boiler as a whole was in a fairly good condition with the exception of the combustion chamber Mr. Howard Smith at his judgment at the inquiry said that having regard to Mr. Senior's long traiuing and experience Mr. Abel was justified in employing him as he did. . .

Peter Lowe. the plaintiff, said he was a water- man. On Septqnber 18th, his son was working for Richard Abel as engine driver on the flat " Amy." He had been working fur Mr. Abel about six weeks previous to his death. He was earning at the time ,about 30s. per week, and it cost about 12i. per week for his board and lodgings.—Cross-examined: Ile had previoualy earn- ed from 234. to 30s. per week when emplayed on the SI ip Canal previous to working for Mr. Abel. Ile w.‘s no employed continuously on the Ship Canal. He estimated that he had lost 18s. per week by his son's death. His 6on never had more than 2s. ed. per week pocket

money. Thomas Gibbons, the captain of the eat" Amy," said that he engaged Ralph Lowe on August 11th. The boiler had been examined by Mr. Senior before Lowe , worked for him. The pressure for the boiler showed 60 lbs. five minutes before the explosion,. Mr. Abel paid witness his wages, and he paid Lowe. He had not been told by either Mr. Abel or hi a clerk to engage Lowe.—Cross-examined : He was free to engage wholn he liked. He made his own arrangement as to wages which were paid. The boiler had been tested alter having new tubes put in on April 16:h and 100 lbs. to the square inch had been applied for ten minutes, and had also Pried a trial trip up the Ship Canal. No com- plaints had been made by Lowe with regard to the boiler. George Bate, engine driver, Runcorn, employed by the Manchester Ship Canal Coy.,said that eight or nine yea; s ago he was on board a tug called Eelfast,as aa engineer. Mr. Abel was the owner. He bad sever lseen engaged by captains on tugs, but only on coasting vessels. When he went on a tug as an engineer the firm engaged him. Mr. Hope said that he submitted that he had no Das* in point of law to meet. lie negligence on the part of his client had been proved. According to the report of the inspector of the Board of Trade, Mr. Senior was pronounced to be quite capable to examine the boiler. His Floneur, however, reminded Mr. Hope that it also said that he was incapable of determining the pressure and had never heard of such expressions as " factors of safety." Mr. Hope said that Senior was an old boiler-maker. He would, however, reserve all further remarks until the end of the case. David Morgan, fitter, lately in the employ of the Ship . Canal Company, said that in April last year he saw the ! boiler of the steam flat " Amy." He tested it by hydraulic pressure to 10Olbe, for ten minutes. It stood the teat satisfactorily. A few days at erwards he and Mr.' Abel and a company went on the SWF) C nal for a trip. The boiler pressure was then 60ibs. 'mere was no symptom of anything being wrong during the trip. Thomas William Dar well, Runcorn, engine driver, said he joined the " Amy " when Mr. Abel purchased her, and was on her Fir nine months. He frequently used between 60 and 651bs. pressure, previous to one of the tubes leaking. Fie assishiel Senior to repair the boiler. He corroborated the last witoess with regard to , the testing of the boiler. Ile again used the same pres- sure as previously after the fie, was again Put out as fit for further work. He drove the engine until mix weeks before the explosion took place. He left because of a dispute, not beeau-e he thought the boat was unsafe.— Cross-examined : He had been engaged be Mr. Abel. It was through a complaint of his that the examination by Senior was made. He told Mr. Senior, who was out of work, that if he saw Mr. Abel he might get the job of repairing the boiler, and that was how Mr. Senior came to get the job. Henry Branch, Weston Point, marine engineer and boiler maker, said that lie had known Seeior for four years, Koh had employed him from time he time to repair boilers and ships !whs. Ile was a very good workman, and anything thaz he had done for withess, he had always done it to his satisfaction. He WHA. competent person to put in a new set of tubes, and to repair boi;ets, and also to inspect the boiler, as to their material durability, but not to 6 the working pressure. j He was quit,: competent h) poi it out any defect in tile plate or stay.—Crns.i-examined : He could not say [het Senior was a Illa II to be engaged to give his opinion it:, to the 'fitness of toilers for work. -He would not employ him as a scientific in:ln.—Re-examined : He considered that Mr. Abel was illStifiea in calling in Senior to ex- amine the boiler. The case was adjourned until next Thursday.

THE CHRISTIAN ENDEAVOUR

MOVEMENT.

MEF:TINGS AT RUNCORN. In Greenway-road Primitive Methodist Chapel, Run- corn, on Wednesday, the first Christian Endeavour convention in Runcorn was held. It was in connection with the Warrington and district union of Young People's Societies of Christian Endeavour, and the meet- ings were well attended. In the afternoon a conference was held, which was preceded by a brief devotional meeting, conducted by the Revs. T. Bramell and J. Stafford. In the absence of the Rev. E. T. Reed, of Warrington, the chair was occupied by the Rev. D. W. , Roberts of Warrington, and he was supported on the platform by the Revs. J. E. Roberts M.A. (co-pastor with Dr. McLaren), T. Bramell, J. Stafford, W. George, and Messrs. A. Mounfield and W. Higginson (Warring- ton). The societies represented at the conference were the Greenway-road EndeavourSociety,St. John's Presby- terian, Halton-road Wesleyan, Ellesmere-street United Methodist, Bethesda Congregational, and Deacon-road Church, Widnes. The Chairman said he thought they had got some good workers in the Christian Endeavour Movement at Run- corn, and th:i societies there, so far as he could gather, were prospering. He brought the greetings of the Warrington societies and wished those at Runcorn many happy returns of the day. He trusted that the year to come would be still more successful, and that their spiritual power would be deepened and that they might do a great deal of good. Proceeding to speak of Christian Endeavour aims ho said it should be clearly borne in mind that the society was not a mutual im- provement society or a debating society. It was not a society for people to air their own opinions and throw them down people's throats. Their society was for the building up of Christian character and teaching its mem- bers how to work for the Master. (Hear, hear.) He then proceeded to speak on the three aims of the move- ment,d welling more particularly on their desire to deepen spiritual life and the training of persons for Christian service. 1 The Rev. J. E. Roberts, president of the Manchester and District inion of the Y.P.S.C.E., delivered an ad- dress on " The Christian Endeavour movement, its prin. ciples and methods." He said the first anniversary ' marked the advance of the movement in Runcorn, and I also showed that one of the mottoes which had been hung up, that they were going forward was true. He hoped that they would go on and prosper. They only wanted a chance of stating what the Christian En- deavour movement meant in order to convert every- body to an enthusiastic belief in its principles and methods. Their principles were really contained in their motto "For Christ and the Church." That was the es- sence of its principles. Its first principle was to win and get young people for Jesus Christ. That was the endeavour it constantly set before its members. It was an organised effort to keep the young people for the Lord Jesus Christ. If they were not there already, to get them,and to keep them true to the pledge that they had taken. It rested upon a spiritual principle,and had a spiritual aitn,and trusted to spiritual methods. It did not despise culture or physical vigour and prowess. The society worked in connection with the church. It could not be a power without the church. It lived with the church and for the church, to help it in its work and try to strengtken it in the efforts it was making in the neighbourhood to spread the principles of Christianity. The great difficulty they had in forming a national society of Christian Endeavour was that they wanted it to be recognised that each society was part of thechurch with which it was formed. A pait of the Endeavour pledge was that the members would make a daily habit of reading the bible and praying. In the weekly prayer meeting they all took some part, and everyone was to be brief and to the point. It was because Christian people had not been taught to give testimony, that the church was weaker. In the Congregational, Baptist, and other churches they had had nothing to take the place of class meetings which were held in cennection with the Methodist churches. But the Christian En- deavour Class helped them to be able to give some sort of testimony relating to their Christian life. At the consecration meeting which was held monthly the re- sponse of each member to the roll call was taken as a renewal of their vow made on becoming an active mem- ber of the society. Their motto and pledge were closely connected. 'the society sought for the active and loving sympathy of the minister and the officers of the church. Any number of committees would be formed, but the three essential were t—rhe look-out committee which introduced new members ; the prayer meeting committee which took charge of the weekly meeting ; and the social committee which was to break down that iceburg feeling so frequent in their churches (applause.) He looked upon the Christian Endeavour Society, and he was an active member of it, as one of the greatest helps to spiritual life, and as a most useful agency for young people in connection with the church. He cordially and lovingly commended the movement to the sympathy of all those ,who had not joined it. (Ap- plause.) The Chairman then spoke on " The Look Out Com- mittee." It was a committee of vital import- ance and which should have very careful consideration. It was the door, by which mem- bers entered the society and the monitor by whom they were admonished. If the committee was efficient the society would be a power for good. The duty of the members of the committee was to be ever on the watch, nothing should escape their attention. A conscientious member must ever b., on the look-out as one who must give account .;)f the important charge committed to his care. The members should be zealous and wise in the performance of their duties. It was bettor to have a few earnest Christian members than 50 half-hearted mem- bers. Mr. Arthur Mounfield, of Warrington, spoke on " The Prayer Meeting Committee " and said too much atten- tion could not be given to .the prayer meeting. It was the life of the society, the common centre, the gathering In which the members strove to gain clearer views of truth and duty, and to gather inspiration for their work. He then explained fully the duties of the prayer meeting committee. Mr. Higginson, of Warrington, next read a paper on the " Social Committee." He said that the movement was one that had comet° stay, and they had dose well in England in welcoming a movement fraught with so much good and which was calculated to do so much for their yong people who were rising to manhood and womanhood. How to retain their elder Sunday school scholars had been a very difficult question for a long time but he believed that the Christian Endeavour Society was a successful attempt to bridge the gap between the church and school. Short testimonies bearing on the work of the com- mittees were afterwards given. Subsequently, tea was provided in the schoolroom when the ladies who presided at the tables were Mrs. Wilson, Mrs. Kilcross, Mrs. Page, Misses Horton, Hex- worth, Shingler, Baker, E. Stables, E. Wright, Lunt, Wilson and H. Wright. After tea, some time was spent in social re-union.
