THE ENENING MEETING.

The church was filled at the evening meeting. The Rev. J. Pulford Williams occupied the chair. After de- votional exercise bv the Rev. J. Simpson, a short report was delivered by Mr. John Wilson. He stated that the Endeavour Society in connection with that church was farmed on February 15th, 1893. Their aim from the beginning waist° get young men and young women con- verted to God. The first business meeting was held on April 1.9:11, 1893, and the membership then stood at 19 active members and 9 associate members. During the first year of their existence,the divine spirit was poured upon them in a most marvellous manner: at one meet- ing eight young people were converted to God and con- Untied in the faith to this day. At the end of the first year there were 42 active members and 34 assoaiate members. Since then other churches had formed socie- ties. The Wesleyan Methodist, Congregational. United Methodist Free Church,Presbyterian Church, and Gospel Mission, had now each a society and were doing a good work amongst their young people. Their report to the Warrington Cenvention this year was 37 active mem- bers, 32 associates, and 8 honorary members. During the society's existence 14 members had joined the church. The church was engaged in mission work and in trying to spread the gospel about them. During the present winter the society had aided this work in various forms and on one occasion they gave a free tea to a large number of poor children. (Applause.) The Chairman said that the Christian Endeavour movement was started by Dr. Clarke, of America. It was marked by success, and its future if mea3ored by the past was one whose influence would not only he felt in this country and the colonies, but would permeate the whole globe. There were three principles that com- mended themselves, namely :—decisioc, service, and deep spirituality. Ttiesociesy was sure to advance be- cause it was a spiritual one. Mr. J. T. Partihgton, of Chester, spoke on "The Model Constitution," which he stated had been carefully thoueht out hy the promoters. it had gained the ad- miration of all grades of Christian men, and -was found to meet all possible wants in connection with the asso- ciations. Some had said that the movement was going to set up a separate church, but this was not the case. Mr. T. Barton Davies, hon. secretary of the Chester and District Union, delivered an address on "The Pledge." The essentia, feature of the Endeavour move- ment, the fundamental principles upon which the whole organisation was based—the pledge indeed was the foundation upon which the whole structure of Christian Endeavour with its wonderful machinery of Christian activity was reared. The real danger the society had to fear was not that the pledge was too severe, but that it might be regarded too lightly, arid the responsibilities it imposed upon the individual with too great laxity. (Applause.) The Rev. E. T. Reed, president of the Warrington District Association, delivered an address on " Our spiritual aims." He said the movement from the be- ginning to the end was a religious movement. The groat aim and object of the society was to bring those who did mot know Christ into a living and vital union with Him, and to deepen the spiritual' life of those who did know Him. The members of the so3iety had one of the erindest works committed to them that it was possible to have, and lie urged them to be real and earnest in seeking by individual contact with the members, and

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT,

FRIDAY. CYPRUS A BAD BARGAIN.

The House of Commons went into Committee of Sup- ply on the Civil Sevice supplementary estimates on Friday afternoon. On a vote for public education in Wales, Mr. ACLAND said the increase of average atten- dance, though steady, had been rather slow. He had nu doubt that the Free Education Act had largely drawn additional children to the schools, and he thought also that education was beceming much more generally ap- preciated in the country. On the vote on account of Cyprus, Sir C. DILE& said the people of the island were very miserable, and their complaints were loud and constant. He had no proposal to mak,l, as to what course the Government ought to take, and should not move a reduction, but should simply say no to the vote. Sir W. FIARIPOURT said the "speculation irt Cyprus" had been most unhappy, but as the British Government had assumed the government of the island they must do their best with it. They would not, however, be justified in doing more for the island than they were doing at p•tssent. At the evening sitting Mr. PROVAND moved a resolu- tion declaring that no system of taxation could be equitable unless it included the direct assessment of the- enhanced value of land due to the increase of popula tion and wealth and the growth of towns. Mr. MOULTON seconded the motion. Mr. KNOWLES said the proposal asked the House to ignore the findings of :he Town Holdings Committee. Mr. SHAW-LEFEVRE said he could not admit the authority of the Town Holdings Commit- tee to be of any great value. He heartily supported the motion, and said the Government hart every sympathy with the case Mr. PROVAND had made. The motion was carried without a division. MONDAY.

In the House of Commons on Monday evening Mr. H. GARDNER in reply to a question gave the figures relating to the existence of rabies in the past six yea's. He said that practically the whole of the increase in 1894 had been in Lancashire and Yorkshire, and the agricul- tural department was in constant communication with those counties as to the measures necessary to bring them into a more satisfactory condition. Mr. ASRUITH, replying to a question, said he agreed that it ought not to be possible to institute prosecutions in such cases as that of the Leeds Sunday lectures, and the Government would look favourably on any well-con- sidered proposal for rendering the law more rational and less open to abuse. Mr. U. GARDNER, in answer to a question, said it was true that the value of butter, cheese, and eggs imported last year from abroad was very nearly £23,000,000. He would be very glad to do anything he could to encourage ' the supply of these articles by home producers. Reports received by the Agricultural Department showed that the largely increased attention which had been given to , technical instruction had resulted in a distinct improve- ' ment in the quality of borne produce. On the order to go into Committee of Supply on the Navy Estimates Mr. ARNOLD-FORSTER moved—" Tha this House, before voting supplies for the maintenance of Her Majesty's naval establishments, seeks an assur- ance that the estimates submitted to it with that object by Her Majesty's Government are based upon consideration of the needs of possible war by sea and land, and upon the consideration of advice tendered in that behalf by the naval officer selected for the conduct of the naval operations in case of war." He said he desired to put a stop to those perpetual scares which were a danger to the country and a disgrace to our naval organization. Sir G. CHESNEY seconded the amendment. Sir E. REED said the proposal was im- practicable. Sir C. MUM, in supportiug the amend- ment, said the Elartington Committee went a long way in the direction of this proposal. Mr. ROBERTSON (on behalf of the Government) said that the estimates had been based on nothing but the consideration of the needs of possible w.ir by sea and land, and that under the present system the First Naval Lord had in his own possession Wails of preparation, organization, and mobilisation, all conceived ssith a view to the needs of a possible war and nothing else, while, moreover, there was in existence a Joint Defence Committee, on which sat the chief official advisers of the Government on naval and military matters. Mr. ARNOLD-FORSTER desired, after Mr. Ronarersox's speecii, to withdraw his amendment, but objection was taken to that course, and the amendment was negatived without a division.

TUESDAY. THE NAVY ESTIMATES.

In the House of Commons on Tuesday, in reply to Sir E. HILL, Sir W. EIARCOURT said he could not see that there was any possibility of exempting credit drapers from the liability to take out hawkors' licenses. The House went into Committee of Supply, and on the vote of officers and men of the navy Mr. BOBERTSON submitted the Navy Estimates for the year. He stated that the actual expenditure under the Naval Defence Act had been £22,241,000, and that all the seventy ships included in the programme were now either actually on service or ready for it. In the present year the Govern- ment were asking for 88,850 men, and the total asked for new construction was £5,393,642. Amongst the new works proposod were the breakwater at Portland, which was to on,t £700,000, a new mole at Gibraltar, costing also £700,000,a torpedo proof harbour at Dover, and an extension of the yard at Hong Kong. The programm. of works previously sanctioned by Parliament repre- sented ahroit, £4,500,000, and the new sthemes an addi- tional £4,200,000, making nearly £9,000,000. which the Government proposed to meet by way of loan. Lord G. HAMILTON considered that the Admiralty had in the statement and estimates laid before the House given an earnest of their determination to do their duty. In the debate which followed [Hr. Fortwoon passed some criticisms on some of the Government proposals. The first vote was passed at midnight, and progress was reported'.

WARRINGTON RAGGED SUNDAY SCHOOL AND Ai resro N.

On Saturday evening last the committee provided another enjoyable evening for the children. Mr. Downs kindly lent the slides, while Mr. George came forward with the use of his lantern. There was an increase of attendance on Sunday after- noon. The "pleasant" Sunday evening was a crnAnt, success. There was a good attendance of children and their parents and friends. Mr. Smith took the chair, and was assisted by Mr. Stuart. of Livnrnool, who delivered a most stirring address which was listened to with rapt attantion. The choir rendered solos and choruses during the even- ing. Mr. Shetland, assisted by the committee, hns been very husy during tile last; two weeks distri- buting clothing to needy rases, and also providing suppers for the children of the school. The com- mittee are anxious that those who take an interest in this acork shimild pay the School a visit, to see the grand work that is being accomplished under very trying and difficult circumstances. The choir of the school has suffered a great loss this week, one of its members and most promi*ing singers has been taken from them. The little singer met with an accident and was burned to death, and the choir which is composed of the poor children wish to pay their humble respect to one whom they loved. BETHESDA "LITERARY AND SOCIAL."

INTERESTING LECTURE BY MR. W. HANDLEY.

A meeting in connection with Bethesda Literary and Social Class was held in the Lecture Room, Runcorn, on Tuesday evening, when a paper was given by Mr. William Handley, entitled " An Afternoon's Ramble," or " Norton and its Priory." In his opening remarks Mr. Handley expressed his pleasure at being enabled to show the practical sympathy with the werk of the class, which reminded him of the first Mutual Improvement Society of the town which claimed for its birthplace the room in Bethesda now known as the Organ Gallery, and many of whose members had since been known as pulpit and platform speakers. Referring to the innate curios- ity which leads men to travel about the earth in search of curiosities, regardless of the fact that some of the finest often lie close to home, Mr. Handley drew the at- tention of his hearers to the fact that within a short walking distance from Runcorn stands the famous Norton Priory, which was abundantly festooned with historical associations of the greatest interest. The now peaceful looking building surrounded by its leafy woods had echoed to the tumult of war and the tread of soldiery, and had also been the quiet haven of good and pious monks whose holy lives were spent within its pre- cincts. The history of the priory and its neighbourhood was mostly ecclesiastical and dated from the Saxon Heptarchy, when, it was recorded, Queen Ethelfleda founded an institution on the site of what is now Run- corn Parish Church. It was probable that from here the monks removed to Norton and established the priory which was richly endowed with lands, but at what date was somewhat uncertain though the remains stamped it as being Early Norman. Of the monks riot much was known, except that they practised the Chris- tian virtues and were the lawyers, sick-visitors, and teachers of the locality. Their patron saint was St. Christopher, the legends referring to whom pourtrayed him as the special saint controlling stormy seas and dangerous fords, and whose statue was still extant at the Priory. In 1423 the Priory became an Abbey and had its own Abbot. During the reign of henry VIII the king looked upen the religious houses of the country as being dangerous to himself and people and therefore determined to put an end to them. Then began a work of spoliation by the Commissioners ap- pointed by the Crown. The monks after strong resist- ance were driven from their happy, home at Norton and thus ended the ecclesiastical hist ,ry of the "Priory." About the year 1545 the house passed into the hands o: the Brooke family in whose possession it had remained ever since, and only upon one occasion, during the Civil War, had its tranquillity been disturbed. The present structure was built in 1780. A " Chester Chronicle "of 1839 recorded the fact than in 1839 Mr. and Mrs. Glad- stone spent their honeymoon at the Priory. In 1865 Sir Richard Brooke, discovered the old Norman doorway in an almost perfect condition and also the " God's Acre " of the Priory where the monks and abbots of long centuries ago had been laid to rest and round which with reverent hands he placed a fence. At the close of the meeting the Rev. J. Simpson on behalf of the class cordially thanked Mr. Handley for the treat he had provided for the delectation of his hearers, in reply to which Mr. Handley said it had given him great pleasure both to prepare and read the paper and if only he had managed to awaken a spark of inter- est in the antiquarian relics of the neighbourhood he would be amply repaid.

lIECSBY.

FIRE AT PROSPECT VILLA.—A fire occurred at the residence of Mr. John Prica, Prospect Villa. Helsby, between six and ten o'clock on Tuesday evening. The occupants of the house were absent at the time, and the outbreak is attributed to a picture in the drawing room, having fallen and upset a lamp, which was smashed to atoms. A great deal af furniture was burnt.. On their return home they found that the fire, which was strictly confined to the drawing room, was almost sut, it having, fortunately, burned itself out. The loss is covered by insurance.

THE GRA.VINU DOCK QUESTION AT RUNCORN.

THE CANAL COMPANY'S REPLY.

Mr. Whitworth, secretary of the Manchester Ship Canal Company has submitted for the consideration of the Runcorn Urban District Council, the pro- posals of the directors of the company relative to the graving dock question at Runcorn. These have not been officially made public, but we believe we I are correct in stating that with one exception, the I proposals are practically in agreement with the counter-proposals of the Rancorn Urban Council. The exception is with regard to the agreement at present existing as to the construction by the Canal Company of a graving dock. Tnis the company wish to shelve altogether whereas the Council pro- posal to waive it for five years. The proposals of the company are in effect that they will provide two ship repairing yards in the vicinity of Old Quay, one being where that of Messrs. Brundritt's existed, and the other on the site used by .Nlessrs. Speakman. These will be thoroughly equipped with steam appliaaces, &c., and certain charges will be made for their use. The slipways will be capable of accommodating vessels of about 150 feet in length. These will be in addition to the exist- ing graving dock near the bonded warehouse. The lay-by accommodation is to be extended to the extent of 650 feet, with a depth of 24 feet of water alongside. Connected with this will he a coal tip for the purpose of loading large vessels. The Com- pany will further erect the bridge over the Bridge- water Canal,at a point near the ( rane in Balton-road, the material for which has already been purchased by the District Council. It is also understood that the Company have promised that the repairing of their craft shall be done in this neighbourhood. These terms were considered at a special m.eeting of the Parliamentary and General Purposes Com- mittee of the Runcorn Council on Monday evening, at the Town Hall, Mr. Philip Speakman, J.P„ C.C., ; occupied the chair. There was a large attendance I of members, but the proceedings were, of course, of I a private nature. After the proposals of the com- pany had been read, a long discussion took place, the meeting being of a protracted character. 'several members objected to the entire waiving of the agreement as to the provision of a graving dock by the company, and as no definite decision ' could be come to, the meeting was adjourned for al week.

SliIP CANAL.

NARROW ESCAPE FROM DROWNING AT SALITORTI . The barque Asia, with a cargo of logs from Mobile, arrived at Saltport at the beginning of the week. Part of the cargo is being sent to Warrington to be stored, whilst the rema.iuing will go on to Manchester. On Tuesday afternoon, three man in the employ of the Man- chester Ship Canal Co., at Saltport, were engaged in packing up drifting logs of timber, and wwre endeavour- ing to pull some away from opposite the tidal opening with the aid of a small boat. When within twenty yards from) the weir the oar broke. The tide at the time was running with great force, and the boat with its occupants was hurled against the wailings of the gan- trey which run over the weir. The men, immediately the boat struck, leaped out and succeeded in reaching the top of the gantrey in safety,but the timber was car- ried into the river,and the boat was overturned and also carried out. Beyond a slight shock the men were little the worse for the adventure.—A young man named Sweetman, mate of a flat belonging to the Manchester Ship Canal Co., also had a narrow escape from drown- ing at Saltport. He was removing a log of timber when his crowbar slipped and he fell overboard into the water. The tug Florida happened to be near at hand, and he was rescued not much the worse for the immersion.

The funeral of the late Mr. Matthew Chorley,who had carried on the business of a plumber and glazier in Warrington for a considerable number of years, and who died at 133. BAPTIST MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT SOCIETY. A paper on "Disestablishmeat" was read at a meeting of the Baptist Mutual Improvement Society, held in the schoolroom, Golborne-street, on Friday evening week. There was a good muster of members, and the chair was occupied by Mr. R. Miller. The essayist was the Rev. D. W. Roberts, who traced the rise and progress of the Established Church, and then proc,,eded to deal with the Church in Wales. He contended that the Church was an alien Church, that it was not attended by the Welsh people, and that its maintenance was an oppressive tia upon the poor Welsh farmers. An interesting discussion arose, the topic chiefly dealt with being that of disendowment. Several of the members did not se the justice of taking the endowments from the Church, but it was pointed out that the endow- ments were given for the benefit of the whole nation, fry! as the Church could no longer be e lrded as a national institution there could be pr rinnustic. in diverting its endowments too'' .•r uses. The following took part in the discussior !esQrs. J. A. Heywood, S. Rickers, W. Hickma . S. Green, W. Patrick, and the Chairman. es of thanks concluded the proceeding. THE PROPOSED CENTRAL POOR-LAW OF.VICES. The sub-committee appointed by the Board c f Guardians, namely Messrs. A. J. Jolley, George Pill- ing, Samuel Davies and William Hickman, o find a suitable site for the proposed central poor-law offices, met at the office of the Clerk to the Board (Mr. James C. Sutton), on Tuesday afternoon. There were also present the Town Clerk and Borough Sur- veyor. It may be remembered that the place sug- gested was Bank House, Sankey-st., with the land at the rear, which many membersof the Board con- sider most suitable. A discussion between the representatives of the Corporation and the Guar- dians took place, and the, site was afterwards in- spected. The sub-committee will report the result of their interview at a future meeting of the Board, and it is believed that if suitable terms can be agreed upon there is every possibility of commo- dious premises, which will include pay stations, i being provided n the near future tor poor-law purposes. The provision of such premises has been frequently discussed at meetings of the Board, and their erection on the site named would supply a long felt want.

" CAPITAL " CHAT.

The approaching retirement of the Speaker ante- dates the period at which it was understood he would lay down the office he has filled for ten years with so much dignity and success. It would be erroneous to say that his task in the chair has been more difficult than that of his immediate predeces- sor,for his predecessor had to preserve order during the long and bitter struggles of the Irish members for the recognition of their principles, when they were still a small but compact band of Ishmaels waging nightly war against Beitish administration. If the sessions have of late years been longer, the hours during which the House has sat daily have, under the new rules, been much shorter; and, in- stead of what was the nightly practice under his predecessor of the House breaking up at three,four, , or five o'clock in the morning,it is seldom now that 'it sits till one. But Mr, Peel has had the arduous task of putting the new rules in operation and of administering them. The old courtly courtesies of debate have disappeared. The tone of the House is not only changed but lowered, and be has had to rule it with a firm and sometimes with a high hand. He has filled the chair in a manter to make his tenure of the Speakership historical,and he will re- tire from his position with the respect of every political party in the House.

If it is a merit for an ex-Chancellor of the Exche- quer to die poor, Lord Randolph Churchill may tairly be said to deserve that praise. No doubt, in leaving personalty to the gross value of seventy-five thousand nine hundred and seventy-one pounds he proves himself to be a richer man than rumour lately made him. But he has not, by his official position, added to his wealth. The sum his father left to him was nearly as large as that which he devises to Lady Randolph for life, to his children afterwards, and, in the event of their death, to ths Blenheim Estate, from which some have thought his father should not, under all the circumstances, have withheld it.

Lord Randolph Churchill was supposed by his admirers to have inherited the mantle of Lord Beaconsfield. Ile has come strangely near to his master in the amount of his gross personalty. Lord Beaconsfield's was proved at seventy-six thousand six hundred and eighty-seven pounds; but the debts to be deducted were exceptionally large—larger than Lord Randolph's are likely to be—some thir- teen thousand pounds. Lord BRaconstield also might be said to die a poor ex-Cbancellor of the Exchequer; for, though his patrimony, was com- paratively trifling (Isaac Disraeli's personalv was twelve thousand pounds), he had a large legacy from Mrs. Brydges Willyams, had larger cheques than the "Daily Graphic's" from Longmans for " Lothair and Endymion," and a large bequest from his wife.

A specimen of the white or square-mouthed rhinoceros (rhinoceros rims), now a practically ex- tinct mammal, has just arrived in LDndon, and is being modelled by Mr. Rowland Ward. It was shot in Zululand by Mr. C. R. Varndell. In Gordon Cumming's time the animal was common.; but the natives of south-east Africa (owing to the excep- tional quality ot the meat) have slaughtered it in large numbers, and the white man, in his unceas- ing rush for gold, Las helped still further to exter- minate it. It is a gigantic pachyderm, second only in size to the elephant.

Mr. Carvell Williams has been most unkrtunate with the Burials Bill. By putting down a motion to refer the Bill to the Standing Committee on Law he has put himself in the hands of his Litterest op- ponents. He was able to get this order discharged, but he cannot, unfortunately, by the standing orders of the House, as far as I can gather, replace his bill in the ordinary pr,sition of a bill standing for the Committee stage except by a special motion. When Mr. Williams attempted to make this motion it was promptly objected to. It is not unlikely that there may he no way out of this dilemma. One result is to deprive Mr. Williams of the right of pre- cedence for the Committee stage of this bill on Wednesdays after Whitsuntide.

Speculation has arisen in scams quarters as to whether Lord Queensberry will be entitled to plead the privileges of the peerage in the prosecution against him at the instance of Mr. Oscar Wilde. Lord Queensberry is not charged with an offence which constitutes felony, but with one which con- stitutes a misdemeanour. This being so, his pri- vilege of peerage will not apply. Had the offence with which he is charged amounted to felony, he would have been entitled to claim trial before his fellow peers. As Parliament is in session the Lord Chancellor would have had to preside, and the case would be decided by the verdict of the majority of the peers. This verdict would not be given, as in the case of an ordinary jury, through the mouth of a foreman, but each peer would declare his decision upon his honour and in presence of the open court, The judgments of "guilty" and 'not guilty "would be collected, reckoned up, and the numbers an- nounced as in &parliamentary division in the tipper House. All this, however, will be unnecessary, as Lord Queensberry's offence comes under the humbler class of misdemeanour. The proceedings will go on as in the case of the humblest commoner, unless, indeed, his Lordship chooses to wear his hat in court, which as a peer he is entitled to do. It is 54 years since a peer was raied for felony, an inter- val long enough to give such a trial nowadays the , character of a new sensation.

The London County Council on Tuesday com- pletely eclipsed the meeting of Parliament in in- terest. The Moderates might have had one of the three chairmen, but they would not take him by way of compromise, and they got none. They brought forward the Duke of Norfolk as a rival candidate to Mr. Arthur Arnold for the office .of chairman, and were beaten, as they knew they would be, by the votes of the non-retiring alder- , men—who are all Progressives. They made a stand also for the vice-chairmanship, and lost; and after an ineffectual attempt to postpone the election of the deputy-chairman, they divided the council on this office also, and were defeated. A compromise Was come 10 with reference to the filling up of the seats of the nine retiring aldermen, whereby each party elected four and the ninth was elecled by common arrangement.

Mr, Worth, the dressmaker, who died on Monday night, was one of the world's great men. His sway was accepted wherever a French dress was worn; although he was a Lincolnshire man. Ile made for all the Courts of Europe, and for the most thickly- gilded ladies of the United States. He cleared on an average eleven thousand pounds by supplying dresses to a certain Fifth Avenue lady. When money was plentiful in the United States, the al- ways interesting Paris correspondent of the "Daily News" tells us, every dress he furnished Co a Royal lady to be worn at a Biwkingbam Palace Drawing Room of the Queen brought telegram orders from all parts of the States where be had customers.who left him their measure of corsages that fitted them exactly.

Worth " suggestionised," and went on a track of his own. On days on which there were to be Court dinners or other functions, ladies who had oriered gowns for them came to him to be dressed. First come, first served ; but there was a wait- ing room with choice refreshments—a very necessary arrangement, for a fair customer might have to wait seven or eight hours. As he liked to mould the figure, be detested padd- ing. Re was a despot, and these who submitted to his despotism paid heavily for it. A plain black silk gown, worth, in regard to the cost of stuff, seven or eight pounds, would cost at Worth's forty pounds, but it was admirably cut, and had style. In five years, 80 rapidly did his business grow, he had six hundred workmen. 1; d had eight hundred and seventy when he died. lie had to buy the whole of the establishment in Rue de In Paix, and then another equally large house in a less leading street for his workroom. There was no better employer than Mr. Worth. He paid liberal wages all round, and was kind and even generous to those in his em- ployment who fell ill. He went direct to the menu- lecturers for all his stuffs.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MARCH 16. 1895.

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAREST NELLIE,

I am awfully glad to tell you that blouses are going to be worn as much this spring as ever, and ft is not too early to begin to get ready for the brighp days which must be coming. Large patterned wool capon is likely to be the favourite material until thinner fabrics can be worn. A pretty and useful blouse for dull days is of coarse harkpatterned °ripen in a warm, but not too bright, shade of red, the small pointed yoke being of satin of the eamo colour covered with thick black lace, and edged with a close box•pleated ruche of inch-wide black satin ribbon. The belt is of widzr ribbon with a large jet buckle, the Gaiibaldi sleeves are finithed with ribbon, and a band of the same passes sound the throat and is tied at the back in a smart bow with outstanding loops. This blouse might be worn with a skirt of any black material from serge to silk,

Another very pretty costume is made of rifle. green cheviot serge, the skirt and cleat strapped and stitched, and finished with buttons to match. The coat will look best with a separate and pro. perly fitting wabtcoat, made of warm lining, with fronts and collar of the serge, just like a man's waiscoat. I know some people think it saves trouble to simulate the waistcoat by joining it into the side seams of the coat, but this plan is really far more treablesome and seldom successful. The stiff shirt front may be either wiAto or pale pink, with a pink tie, and the smart little toque has a 'white satin crown, covered with jetted net, and a folded brim of rote pink vriTet, caught with a tiny jet or crystal buckle in irons, and finished with a jet ornament. Odd piecee of satin or brocade come in bcaut;fully for crowns, end may be embroidered with the j,t if preferred- Ihis 'Li Vll3 of the very daintiest toques I have seen, and I meet describe it to you. The full crown verse of marigold velvet, embrotdered with small jet paillettes, and the brim of black velvet with a jet buckle in front, two little black tips curling softly over the crown lib the side, and a large cater, or rather spray, of dark violets aid leaves resting on the hair at the back. T e violets harmenised well with the velvet and the dress, which was of a pansy shade; but if a blue dress is worn the violets must be relinquished. Black silk holds its own, and appears on nearly every chapeau, while bright rosapink vies with the übiquitous violets for populazity. I have seen a geed many roses about, but they are Dot spree- Priate to the season, but the moderate sized °hoax rosettes are much prettier. Fashions are just now dreadfully slack, and Isbell lime to wait till the spring things come in before I can give you sny idese es to bail.

For a little girl of six to 10 t i 3 is a simple little frock, Just a simple little sku out in two parts, lined throughout and trimmed •,,,;th a band of silk or velvet. To this is sewn the ro.) ld bodice, which has the material arranged foil over the lining in front, the book being quite plain and fastening with buttons. The neck is fizished with a collar of silk, and a sash of the same Is tied round the waist.

Do you know a dish called npricob fancies? It Is very easily made hut wry delicious. Place in a glass dish six or ?nor:, email round sponge cakes, nicely moisten them with the syrup from apricots preserved whole, diluted with a little sherry; on the top of each c:ica place a preserved apricot, and over the whole spread an icing, prepared from proper icing sugar mlxod with the white of an egg ; this will set tirat, and h a great attraction to little people. Pieserved fruit, is exo3llent for this dish.

Yours lovingly. JENNxn.

About seven and a-half millions of tons of coal are annually consumed in London. Eight hundred and thirty-eight pairs of corsets for men were raade by one firm in England last year. Wives are bought and sold in China. Twenty years ago the average price was five pounds. Now it is fifty pounds, and parents, realising that there is a fortune in a family of daughters, strangle fewer of their female children at birth.

In Vienna a ssrvant girl is not permitted to climb on the window-sill to clean windows without a safety-belt and rope attached to it which fastens to the building, so that in case she should slip or lose her balance she is sus- pended in safety, and can be drawn back into the window.

Noticing a friend's windows the other day, how bright and clean they were, I most anxiously inquired how they had been cleaned, as mine had been lett two weeks owing to the frost. She said, " I use a rag dipped in paraffin oil—nothing else ; and,what is better, the windows are never frozen after." This I have since found to be perfectly correct. The jumping-jack is by no means an invention of to-day, for the early Egyptian children had it among their playthings. In the Leyden Museum there is a capital little figure of a man working away, even as a currier or a baker. There is a sloping block nefore him. You pull the string and the figure moves, the hands keeping on the slope of the block. Mr. Story, the sculptor, who began life as a lawyer, tells a good anecdote which illustrates the fact that the emphasis which punctuates has as much to do with de- termining the sense of a sentence as the meaning of the words. Once, when he was called upen to defend a woman accused of murdering her husband, he adduced as one of the proofs of her innocence the fact of her having att&ded him on his death-bed, and saying to him, when he was dying, "Good-bye, George !" The counsel for the prosecution declared that ought rather to be taken as a proof of her guilt, and that the words she had ußed were, "Good, by George !" The frowsy old feather bed of our infancy has so com- pletely, gone out of favour that it is hardly, necessary to place one more stone on the cairn of abuse already raised over it by doctors and nurses. If the springs are not adopted, a couple of thick mattresses one of horsehair and one of wool, will stake as soft a Led as anyone need wish for. Never place abed against the wall of,a new house. , The damp i& certain to come out sooner or later. If the room is draughty, utilise screens, which are far more healthier than the out-of-date curtains. They form, besides, an excellent shield from the .light in sum- mer, and always add to the artistic appearance.

How to Find Sleep. America is always finding out new ways of doing old things. How to get sleep is a question which has had many answers, from washing one's band to picturing sheep jumping over a hedge, but an American doctor fairly opens our eyes by his recipe for closing them, and falling into the arms of Morpheus. He asserts that the would-be sleeper was simply to lessen the supply of oxygen to the lungs. This produces slight asphyxia, the heart and the circulation become calmer, the brain loses the exciting pewer which has worried it and kept it active, and sleep follows as a matter of course. How can we limit the supply of oxygen,/ In two or three ways. We can throw the bed clothes over our heads and breathe confined air. It is true that is is not a good to take this carbonic acid into the system, but we do not , long remain covered up. We instinctively throw off the cover when we are asleep. The same object can be ob- tained by burying our faces in the pillows. The author of this method points to the dog and cat in support of the correctness of his statement—" How do these animals prepare for sleep ? " he asks. " They turn round Pnce or twice, then settle down with their noses buried in their furry coats. Imitate them." The method is decidedly original, but does it not belong to the kill or cure treatment ?

How, the German Emperor was Married : Singular Statements. .

The brilliant writer-7--;:-AU-icdotal Europe" gives the following piquant particulars of aril episode in the life of the present Kaiser. He says that a clause in the new law against the Socialists, which renders it illegal to discuss marriage from the subversive point of view, has raised a hornets' nest about the Emperor's ears. " Candidly speaking, one cannot but say,' Serves him right ! ' With such a mother and two such grand- mothers as his, he ought to have considered twice before hampering women in their crusade against the domes- tic tyranny of the German autocrat by courtesy called a husband.' We will leave Queen Victoria out of the debate, inasmuch!as her influence ever her grandson was never directly exercised until he had reached a man's estate, but we are not prepared to be equally re- ticent with regard to his mother's and parental grand- mother's influence. But for these two true and good women, Emperor Wilhelm might not be alive to-day ; his fate might have been that of Rudolph of Hapsburg; in other words, be might have become the victim of some abominable intrigue, which if it had not ended in midnight assassination,would haveleft him unfit to com- plete the noble task bequeathed to him by his grandfather and father. For there was a time when the young Prince was very near falling into the trap laid for him by an adventuress; there was a time when he bounded into his father's room and announced his intention of foregoing his prospective inheritance—the imperial crown—of re- tiring into private life, and of marrying an opera-singer. If my memory does not play me false, her name was Carlotta Groesi. The father sat absolutely thunder- struck ; not so the mother. In spite of the lateness of the hour, she simply put on her bonnet and cloak, and five minutes later she was seated by the side of her father-in-law, who had already retired to bed, and told him everything. Next morniug Prince Wilhelm was shown an album containing a dozen portraits of German Prin- cesses, and given half-an-hour to make choice of a wife. It is said that he refused, and told his mother that she could choose for him. The Crown Princess took her son at his word, with what result all the world knows : the chose a sensible,domesticated German Princess. one who was not likely to protest much against the young Prince's somewhat autocratic disposition, who has made him an excellent wife, through whom Germany is perfectly secure with regard to succession. The Empress of Ger- many is perhaps not an ideal woman,but she was chosen by a woman who had never know the galling yulte of of matrimony as it is understood by Germans in general. For that alone, the German Emperor should not curtail discussion of the marriage tie on the part of the en- lightened women in his empire."

THE UNEMPLOYED.

The Parliamentary Committee on the Unemployed met on Monday to consider the draft interim report prepared by the Chairman (Mr. Campbell-Bannerman). The re- port referred to the evidence already placed before the Committee with respect to London, stated that the Corn- .nittee did not feel qualified at this stage of the proceed- ings to make any specific recnomendations. and sug- gested that the Committee should continue its invest iga. tions. A number of amendments was proposed, includ ing a motion for the rejection of the report, but ultim atelv it was agreed to without any material alteration

WE PARTED AT THE ALTAR;

Br LAURA JEAN LIMEY,

Author of "A Mad Betrothal," "lone," "Parted by Fate," "Floribere Lover," etc., etc.

• CHAPTER IX. A PINE LOOKING COUPLE. Doris, almost fainting from excitement, stood fairly rooted to the pavement while the phaeton whirled past.

A lady and gentleman walking directly in front of her turned and. looked admiringly after the

natty ecluipage and its handsome occupant& "A remarkably fine-looking couple," she heard the gentleman remark. And in answer to the lady's question if he knew them he replied: "'there are very few New Yorkers who do not.

She is the 'daugler of a wealthy gentleman who lives somewhere in the suburbs. She's only a school-girl as yet. Home just now on a vacation from boarding•school, understand., Her com- . . .

panion is Banker Thornton's sou. I had it from his own lips yesterday that the rumour floating about is perfectly true. He is soon to marry Miss

Courtney."

Without pausing to hear another word, Doris turned like one who had been dealt a sudden death.-

blow, and fled pr:cipitately down the street. There was a small park at the end of it, which she entered, and flinging herself down on one of the benches, sobbed as though her heart would break. Then she dashed the tears from her eyes, and laughed aloud, and the sound of that wild laughter startled her.

"He is soon to Mary Miql Courtney 1 That is what the gentleman said 1" she cried, putting the shining hair back from her flushed, tear-stained face. For one little minute I almost believed it,

It is utterly impossible, absurd Why, he could not marry her, for he is married to me. No one else could claim him. No one else has a right by his side." .

She wondered if she should have called out to him. All her pride rose up and answered, "No I"

"What do other wives do when their husbands forsake them?" she muttered, below her breath.

"I have heard of such things, but I never quite believed a husband who took the trouble to marry a young girl would heartlessly, deliberately desert her. I never dreamed that would be my fate. I never thought what those wives did. Oh! if I had a mother to go ta— a sister to sympathise with me. It is more bitter than death to be all alone in the cold, cruel world."

Sitting there, listening to the falling, splashing water of the fountain, the thought occurred to her again as to what she should do—where she should go. She could not sit in the park much longer— already the policeman patroling the grounds was commencing to eye her sharply. She might walk through the crowded streets until night set in—but where could she go after that? She was quite too dazed to think. Slowly she arose and moved toward the entrance gate.

For long hours Doris paced the streets of New York until the dusk began to'gather and the great electric lights were lighted. At length the strength of her limbs seemed to fail her: glancing up, she found herself standing before the entrance of the same park in which she had sat that morning. She tried to reach the bench by the fountain. She took one step within the enclosure; then, with a little faint cry, she threw up her hands and fell, face downward, on the gravel walk in a dead faint. In a moment the same policeman who had watched her so furtively that morning hurried to her side. "Poor child," he muttered, "so young and so fair. lam sure she is a stranger in New York. It would be a deed of mercy to take her home with me and let Ellen attend to her." And without further ado Doris was conveyed across the way to the patrolman's humble home. When Doris opened her eyes she found herself lying on a chintz covered settee, and a kind, motherly face bending over her, and the look of puzzled wonder deepened in Doris's pansy blue eyes. • "You fainted, my dear at the park gate, and my husband brought you here," explained the little woman. "You have been here all night. Your friends will be greatly worried over you, I fear." "Friends I" repeated Doris, drearily. "I have no friends here ; not one." "Where are you stopping ?" asked the patrol- man's wife, gently. "lam a stranger in the city; I just came here," answered Doris. "Did you come to New York to find work, poor child ?" asked the womn. Doris raised her eyes to the plain, kindly face. " I—l have had a great trouble," she said—" so great that it has blotted everything else out of my mind. I quite forgot to think out the great problem of life—what lam to do." "Pool- child,!" sighed the woman, "you have had a great trouble, and you so young. You can't be much over seventeen, I'm sure." , "Just a few months past,' said Doris, wearily. "I have a daughter, my dear, so much like you, fair of face, with shining golden hair. She has married and gone away out West, far away from us, and you touch my heart because you are like her." "Married 1" repeated Doris, bitterly. , loved her why did you let her marry?—" The patrolman's wife laughed a cheerful little ' laugh. "Why did she marry, my dear? Why, because she loved her husband. Heaven intended them for each other, or they would not have wed." "Does Heaven always intend the two who marry for' each other?' asked Doris, breathlessly. "To be sure, my dear," replied the woman, wondering at the question. "But supposing, by any chance, people find out they have made a mistake in marriage—that they have married the wrong person—what do they do then ?" Do, my dear?" replied the good woman in wonder. "How do you mean ?" "How do they remedy the mistake?" ,Doris asked. "Do many men desert their wives I"

"If you

• "Oh, dear, no," was the quick reply. " Once married, married for ever. There. is iso_belp for

such a mistake. If people are dissatisfied after marriage they rarely complain. They sensibly make the best of it." Doris turned her white face to the wall. " Oh, my God r she thought, in the bitterness of her own thoughts, "I cannot understand the problem of my own life. If he meant to marry me, why did he desert me ?" That was the burden of her passionate cry. Doris drank the cup of tea that was brought her, but she could take no food.

"1 am sorry," she said ; "you are very kind to me, hut I cannot eat. Let me thank you before I go." "Are you going out to try to find work, my dear ?" asked the good woman, wistfully. " Yes,' said Doris, bravely. " Then I should advise you to buy a thick veil for yourself the first thing you did. I say it only in kindness. Beauty like yours is a dangerous and fatal gift often to a young*girl. You will remem- ber my words when you are older. They will come hack to you many a time through life. I would advise you to get a veil at once to hide such a face as that from the gaze of men: A pretty face °ilea hrings misery when its owner is unprotected and innocent, as you appear to be." A few minutes later Doris was out in the crowded street again At the first draper's shop she passed Doris went in o purchase a veil. A gentleman, tall, dark, and handsome, stood at the counter, looking over an assortment of kid gloves. He glanced up as Doris entered, and the glove he held dropped to the show-case. . "What an.exquisite face !" he muttered, glanc- ing furtively at the lovely face fair and dainty as a flower, crowned in its sheen of curling golden hair, the great, childish, pansy-blue eyes, and the lovely rosebud mouth. "I would like to look at some veiling," said Doris, timidly approaching the counter. "We do not keep veiling here," responded the clerk, mentally wondering why young girls with such pretty faces could be induced to wear a veil at any price. "You'll probably get what you want at Kellogg and King's." "I do not know where that is," said Doris. "I —l—am a stranger. I have just come to New York." As she spoke, as though attracted by a magnet, Doris glanced up, and encountered the fixed gaze of a pair of burning dark eyes. • Blushing crimson, she knew not why, Doris dropped the sweeping lashes over her eyes, turned hurriedly, and left the

"No doubt she has come to New York to look for work," said the clerk, turning to his other cus- tomer. "It's a sad pity young girls will come to this over-crowded city. It's a shame to see such a pretty little creature adrift on the world." Making some casual reply to the clerk, the gentlemanly, handsome young man walked out of the shop, quickening his pace as he reached the pavement, hurrying after the retreating little figure up the street, Doris had pnrchased the veil, and only the long, golden curls were visible and the prettiest dimpled chin imaginable. "I must buy a paper and look it over," thought Doris. And acting upon the thought', she bought a paper, and the first line that met her eye was the advertise- ment of an agency that furnished employment to

women.

Doris made her way there without delay. There was a lady superintendefit in attendance, who looked curiously at the fair young face as Doris paid her fee. • "Your name?" she said, posing the pen in her white fingers. , "Miss Thorne," Doris replied. And it almost seemed to Doi is other lips than her own must im.ve 'framed that answer when she , looked back at that memorable scene in the bitter, tragic after years which followed. She gave her age as seventeen, adding, earnestly, she should like to get a position as governess, if she could.

"That is very young," returned the lady. "A governess of seventeen stands but a poor chance. Of course you have good references?" she added. "No," replied Doris, in a low voice. "I am an utter stranger to the city." The gentlemanly stranger had entered the agency, standing unobserved behind Doris while this con- versation was going on, and now _he retreated to the door.

"No reference'!" said the manageress, raising her eyeb:ows. "Surely you must know someone who can vouch for your respectability. Requiring a reference is customary,' she explained, seeing Doris draw back with wounded pride and blush to the roots °Hier lovely, golden hair. "I can give none,' she answered, simply. "Then I am obliged to return your fee and take your name from our books," :returned the lady. And faint and dizzy, Doris walked out of the agency. As she reached the pavement, her eyes blinded: by tears, some one touched her arm, and, glancing! up, Doris found the dark-eyed stranger she had, met in the draper's shop bowing low before her.

CHAPTER X.

PRAYER ANSWERED.

Dome drew back a little startled. The hand- some' dark-eyed stranger she had first encoun- tered in the draper's shop was standing before her, bowing most lA, undly, as she stepped out of tae emplvyment agency, discouraged and sick at heart _ _ . _ _

_ " I beg your pardon, Miss,' he said, respectfully lifting his hat. "I was standing in the doorway of the agency a moment since, and could not help hearing all that passed between the manageress and yourself. lam in search of some trustworthy young lady to fill a position recently and un- expectedly vacated. You are in search of a situation; suppose you try the one I have to offer."

Poor at% Doris stood dumb and transfixed with joy before him. Surely God must have heard the prayer that had just pile up to heaven to direct her to some way by which she could earn her daily bread.

It never occurred to her guileless, unsophisti- cated heart to mistrust him. She implicitly be- lieved God had sent him in answer to her prayer. That this was simply an excuse to speak to her she 'had never once dreamed. She had not come to that knowledge of the world which teaches that men's lips often speak one thing and their hearts another. _

She never suspected there was anything unusual in this procedure, or that the handsome stranger would not have asked the, same question of any other young girl whom he chanced to meet on the steps of the employment bureau. Doris clasped her tiny white hands suppli- catingly before her as she replied, with trembling eagerness, straight from the depths of her heart : "I think God must have sent you to me, sir, for I was just praying that I might find some posi- tion at once. I—l do not know very much—but I should try, oh! BO hard to please you, sir, if you would only trust me with the position. I am so glad you met me instead of some one else. I did not know which way to turn. lam a stranger In gew York." "Any one could readily see that," he answered, with a light laugh, that somehow jarred harshly upon Doris. "What is the position, sir?" she asked, timidly. ".els clerk in a retail goods store up town," he answered. • "Remain just where you are until I summon a cab," he said, turning away. "I won't be long." He had barely stepped to the edge of the pave. ment ere the manageress of the agency, with a white, anxious face, called to Doris. "My child," she said, leaning over her desk, "would you mind telling me what that gentleman was saying to you? My motive for asking you you shall know hereafter." In a few brief words Doris related the conversa- tion verbatim. _ .

" I thought as much," replied the manageress, her face dark with anger. Would to Heaven we could find a law to punish such wretches. That man is well-known; a greater rascal does not walk the streets of New York. Young girls should never speak to strangers," she went on, as she gazed at the lovely young face. "Many a trap is set for the feet of the innocent and unwary in gx-lb, wicked city like New York. You niust leave here at once," she said, thoughtfully, "before he comes back, and remember—l give you my best advice when I say, flee from that man as though he were accursed. He is not a man for you to know or trust. I almost ask it as a prayer—for your sake, mind." "I will," replied Doris, sobbing out her grati- tude. "I think I will break from our general rule, in this one instance, and help you to a situation, child."

As she spoke she hurriedly wrote down the addresses of three or four ladies whose names were on her books, in want of a governess for children. "I sincerely hope you will be successful with some one of these,' she said, handing the card to Doris.

moment later, dazed and bewildered, Doris found herself on the street again. She had scarcely proceeded half a block ore she found herself face to face with the handsome stranger again. • "Where now, my pretty .one ? Where are you going, I say ? " he asked, laying a detaining hand on her arm. "You did not wait for me to return." For one moment Doris's heart almost ceased beating as she gazed up into the dark, angry face, lit up by the bold black eyes that were regarding her so intently. "I—l have changed my mind," she said, desperately, as she shook off the touch of that white hand from her arm; "I do not want the position." Some one has been putting nonsense into your head 1" he exclaimed, angrily. "1 won't be trifled with in this way. Come 1" "Sir 1" cried Doris, hatlghtily, "how dare you speak so to me? lam young, but I am no coward, sir 1" she retorted, spiritedly. "You cannot compel me, and you have no right to urge me to accept a situation I do not wish. Let go my arm instantly and lot me pass !" she panted. "Dear me 1" he cried, "what a little whirl- wind I By George, now, whenever a man tells me women lose their beauty when they are thoroughly angry, thap man and I will have a subject to argue

• ' On. In a flash Doris flew past him, hurrying down the thoroughfare panting and excited. The thril- ling event which had just transpired had at least taught her a severe lesson; she must have nothing to say to strangers. "There seems to be so little faith that can be placed in any one in this world," she sobbed, drawing her veil closer over her tear-stained face. Doris read the three names on the card. "I will go to the one whose name is on the list first. No doubt that is nearest." She had some difficulty in making oat the right car which she was to take; but at length she reached Mrs. Longworth's elegant mansion on Fifth avenue. LATCHFO RD.

CLAIM FOR DAMAGES AT THE COUNTY COURT. At the Warrington County Court, on Thursday, last week before his Honour, Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, John Tickle, green rocer, Frederick-st., Latchford, sued John Hamblett foe£so,for injuries alleged to have been receiv- ed by a collision in Latchford, between the horses and traps of the respective litigants. THE INFLUENZA SCOURGE.

NEWS AND HINTS. HOW TO FACE THE EPIDEMIC.

The Epidemic of Influeaza is not yet at an end : and the after-effects of this terrible disease are still felt by numerous sufferers. Indeed, severe as inflidenza is in its attack, the after-symptoms are perhaps even mora insidiously dangsrous, and it behoves all who have suffered from the dreaded epidemic to safeguard them- selves against its later consequences.

The Scarborough Mercury describes an event of local interest, which bears out this opinion. A reporter of the great newspapermamed interviewed a Mr. Thomas Harland of 10, Arncliffe-terraip,' Glaisdale, Yorkshire, who had been rendered incapable of work by Influenza duriug a previous epidemic. INtr. II arland said, in reply to questions, " My illness began a year ago last Christ- mas with a severe attack of Influenza. Afterwards I began rapidly to get weakar ; I could not sleep at nights, my appetite failed, and although I did not take to my bed, yet I could scarcely support my own weight. I tried all sort of doctoring.' I was a month in the Scarborough Sea Bathing Infirmary ; then I went to Harrogate, and the doctor told ma that I was suffering from anemia, or bloodlessness, in consequence of In- fluenza, which affects the nerves and causes great general weakness. I returned home still very weak,and utterly unable to be of any use. Just at this tune, how- ever,;my son saw an accent inn one of the papers of a woman who had suffered from Influenza like inyself,and who told a reporter what marvellous effect Dr. Williams , Pink Pills for Pale People had had upon her. I determin- ed to try them. I could not tell that I had received any relief from the first 13)x, although I was told I was looking b2tte,!; bat Lyfo" I hAfl taken a second box I found myself rapidly improving. I was able to sleep better, my nerves were stronger, and my appetite much better. New, after my fifth box, I am quite strong again, and I strongly recommend them to all who suffer."

In all cases of Influenza and after effects, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have a miraculous efficacy. They are recog- nised as one of the greatest modern medicines,a perfect blood builder and nerve restorer, and an unfailing cure for rheumatism, sciatica, neuralgia, partial paralysis, locomotor ataxy, St. Vitus' dance, nervous headache, prostration, Asc. They restore pale and sallow complex- ions to the glow of health, and are a specific for all the troubles peculiar to the female sex ; while in the cases of men they effect a radical cure in all cases arisingfrom Imental worry, overwork, or excess. Those Pills are sold by all chemists, or post free from Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, of 46, Eiolborn-via- -11 duct, London, at 2s. 9d. a box, or six boxes for 133. i/d. IDr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People are never sold loose, and substitutes in this form are useless and fraudulent. The wooden box must be in a pink wrapper with full xante, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People.

ANTI-VACCINATRIN MEETING AT WARRINGTON.

A meeting promoted by the Anti-Vaccination Society, was held in the Co-operative Ball, Sankey-st., Warring- ton on Monday evening. There was a crowded audience, and the chair was occupied by Mr. F. Monks, J.P., CC. The speaker for the evening was Lieutenant General Phelps,of Birmingham, who chose as his subject " Vaccination at work." The address was illustrated by limelight views, the lantern manipulator being Mr. Wilkinson.

• The CHAIRMAN, in opening the meeting observed that although he was in the chair that evening it must not be concluded that he was an anti-vaccinist. He looked at the question very much in this way, that Parlfament in its wisdom had passed a law for the regulation of this matter, and personally he obeyed that law. He did not object to vaccination, and consciously he had no grounds upon which to base any objection; but he recognized that there were always two sides to a ques- tion, and that in the question before them there was a great deal to be said on both sides. (Applause.) General Phelps would tell them, no doubt, that vaceina- tion did not do that which it professed to do. Heresy had a perfect right to exist ; it existed in matters of theology, and why should it not exist in medical science ? In this country we were all free to hold our own opinion so long as we were all sincere in seeking after truth. (Applause.) In conclusion, the Chairman intimated that General Phelps would answer any written question, but it must be understood that the meeting was not one open for public discussion. The promoters had organized it for the health of the town, and it was optional for the other side to hold one to advocate their views. Then, after the meeting had been held, and if any discussion occurred, the society with which General Phelps was associated (the Birmingham Anti-Variole League for the suppression of compulsory vaccination and the sub- stitution of sanitation for cow-pox) would be quite willing to take their full share in that discussion. (Ap- plause.) Lieutenant General PHELPS then proceeded with his lecture. He describe(' vaccination as one of the great fallacies which the present generation of medical men had fallen into. It was quite a mistake to think that medical men were incapaele of error. It was not long since they denounced mesmerism. Now they had adopted it under the brand new name of hypnotism,and wished to make it the special privilege of medical gentle- men to use it. Was the idea of vaccination which meant giving disease to prevent disease, a scientific one ? He said it was not. Unfortunately at the present time, the medical world had an unconscious bias which affected all that it recorded during a small-pox epide- mic. In Birmingham be found great diffi- culty in getting the addresses of those persons who, it was stated, had died unvaccinated. He was glad to say, however, that Dr. Gernan, of Warrington, had recorded almost all the names and addresses of such persons, and he hoped that they would be investi- gated. rho lecturer here gave several examples of cases which had been entered as unvaccinated in Bir- mingham, and yet when investigated proved to have been vaccinated. The cause of the doctor's mistake was the absence of marks. The absence of marks was en- tirely deceptive, for in persons with peculiar kinds of skin, the marks disappeared after a time. The lecturer at this point threw on the screen a large number of photographs of diseased animals, and also oh children whose bodies were covered with filthy scabs and ulcers, alleged to have been caused by vac- cination. The pictures were certainly of a kind which no person of sensitive feeling could look at. He con- tended that in places such as Leicester and Keighley, where the vaccination law was practically in abeyance, the death rate bad largely decreased, and he showed diagrams to prove that vaccination failed to arrest smallpox. In Warrington thee- were still behind other towns in sanitary matters. Ho had read Dr. Gornall's report, in which some valuable suggestions were made. There were some people who thought themselves very clever in keeping down the rates, but if they kept them down at the expense of sanitation and cleanliness they spent a pound for every shilling they saved. de con- tended that it was better sanitation,and not vaccination which caused smallpox to decrease. The English Parliament had in a moment of mistaken confi- dence,passed the vaccination law, whereby parents who refused to allow their children to be vaccinated were liable to fines or imprisonment. That was un-English. they in Warrington were wise, they would at once term an anti-Vaccination Society and obtain the power for parents to have their children treated as they pleased. The parents were responsible for their child- ren's development, and they should therefore have the right to say whether the poison should be introduced into their children's system or not. By doing so, they would be saving the lives of their children just as they were doing successfully at Leicester. (Applause.) The CHAIRMAN then asked if any one would like to put any questions to the lecturer. Two persons handed written questions, but neither were of public impor- tance. Mr. Jotter PEERS said that he had great pleasure in proposing a vote of thanks to the lecturer. He was sure that they had increased their knowledge upon the subject of vaccination. The diagrams they had seen that night bad been very distressing. They were most unpleasant, and he hoped that they would do something towards making them more icclined te keep their telood pure. The Motion was 'seernaded, and the Chairman before putting it to the meeting said that he thought that, as i far as his knowledge of Warrington went,it was quite in accord with the general opinion. They had had to pay a heavy rate in putting down disease, but it was satis- factory to he able to say that it was not without suc- cess. When he was first appointed to the Sanitary Committee of the Town Council, the death rate was over 33 per cent., and he was glad to say that during the years he was on that committAi—and he bore will- ing testimony to the work of Mr. John Burgess,although he had had as much to say against that gentleman as anybody—that they never hid a stronger or more reso- lute advecete of the measures for increased sanitation than Mr. John Burgess. The motion was carried unanimously, and the lecturer briefly replied, speaking in commendatory terms of the report of Dr. Gorna.ll and also of Ws sug- gestions for the t erment of the borough. He said, however, that no ai,letint of lilth and dirt in teNe town, was as dangerous as dieease and dirt which P 713 intro- duced into the bleed. (Hear, hear and applause.) Dr. J. GUEST GORNALL said that there was another side to the vaccination criotion,an.l ho intended shortly to lecture upon the subject. He hoped that tinge present would come again, and listen as patiently tn him as to Lieutenant-General Phelpo. (Bear, hear and applause.) The meeticg then terminated.

THE EKAMIN.I4,R, SATURDAY, MARCH 16, 1895.

REVOLTING CASE OF CHILD NEGLECT

AT WARRINGTON.

PARENTS SENT TO PRISON.

On Friday morning last week at the Borough Police Court, before Alderman Wright and other maeistrates, John and Sarah Ann Fid- dler, 188, Pierpoint-st., were charged with having on the sth of March, and on diversdates during the last six months, unlawfully neglected their child Annie Fidd ler, in a manner likely to injure her health. Defendants pleaded not guilty. Mr. Percy Davies, solicitor,prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C.' and said that he thought the Bench would agree with him that if any case deserved punishment this did. When it was considered that the times were hard for deserving poor, these people came before them presumably as poor people, but he had to disclose to them that the man had lace!' in receipt of some 253. per week to keep his home, consisting of himself, wife and four children. At the present time there were only two children living in the house, as the eldest is on the " Clio " and the second in an industrial school and the children have gone to those institutions through the neglect of the defendants. It was a fact that the defendants had been up at court previously for neglect and in the present case such a scene of squalor had never been seen in Warrington before. The woman was well-known as a drunken woman and she had allow- ed the child which had been severely scalded about the lower part of its body and legs to be in the house for 17 days without its being attended to, and the child ran a very great danger of death from its injuries. He thought their worships,when they heard the circumstances, would agree with him that it was a very bad case and he was instructed to press it most strongly as the defendants could have no possible feeling for their children. Inspector McAteer said he went in company with Sergeant Taylor to defendant's residence at 3 o'clock on the afternoon of Tuesday, and when he got inside, after some delay, he found the father and mother upstairs. There was nothing ir.the shape of furniture there but a small bed, and the rooms were filthy in the extreme. Below there was no furniture but a table, and not a chair in the house. The child was lying on the bed, and when witness examined it, it was naked except for a dirty chemise, and there was no other clothes to put on. It was suffering from burns or scalds on the abdo- men and the thighs, and legs from the knee downwards. He asked the mother when the child was hurt, and she said "last Saturday afternoon week was about 11 o'clock in the morning." She had not called in any doc- tor as she did not think it necessary. The male defen- dant said his wife had done all she could for the child. Witness insisted on the child being removed to the In- firmary, which was done in an old dirty sheet. The woman, however, would not allow her child to stop there, and he was instructed to press the case. Since he had COMP to Warrington, 12 months ago, he had re- peatedly called at defendant's house, and on every occa- sion he had cautioned them about their responsibility. Four or five times when he called the door had been closed when he w.derstood the woman was in. P.S. Taylor stated that when he accompanied the last witness he had P.C. Roper with him,and they arrived at the house of defendants about 2.15 and were knocking until nearly three o'clock. They made inquiries and were told that the child was lying there very badly burnt, and he then determined to get in some way. Going round to the back Roper lifted him up and leaning through a window he managed to unbolt the door, and they went in. The win- dows were broken and also the doors. He did not think there was a proper door in the house. He agreed with last witness in his particulars, and added that the closet I tub was in the house, increasing the state of filth. Dr.Langdale, house surgeon at the Infirmary,described the injuries of the child as it appeared when brought to him on Tuesday. With reference to itslving at home 17 days he thought it ran a risk of death from neglect, and it was the duty of the parents to have procured medical assistance. It would have also been better for the woman to have allowed the child to remain at the Infirmary. _ _

Mr. IL Taylor, Police Court missionary, knew the de- fendants,and said the man worked hard,but the woman he could say nothing for. Mr. Isherwood, landlord of the house, said that it was one of 14, and on the lot in the last four years he had spent £4oo,and two years ago he had had a new inside put in the image of the defendants. Mr. D. Tinnion said that the male defendant had worked for him for some years and he had always found him a steady and hard-working man. He had never seen him the worse for drink and he earned on an average 235. a week. Witness had known defendant send home all his money on Saturday and while he was in full work his wife had gone regularly to the soup kitchen. Eventually, the Chairman of the bench thought the least they could do was to commit the woman for one months' hard labour, but so far as the father was concerned they would let him *ff with 14 days hard labour.

COLERIDGE'S ELOQUENCE.

"I was going," says Charles Lamb, " from my house at Enfield to the India Houseone morning, when I mat Coleridge on his way.to pay ma a visit. Ele was brim- ful of some new idea, and—in spite of telling him that time was precious—he drew me within the door of an unoccupied garden by the roadside,and there, sheltered from observation by a hedge of evergreens, he took me by the button of my coat, and, closing his eyes, com- menced an eloquent discourse., waving his right hand gently as the musical words flowed in an unbroken stream from his lips. I listened entranced ; but the striking of a church clock recalled me to a sense of duty. I saw it was of no use to attempt to break away, so, taking advantage of his absorption in his subject. and with my penknife quietly severing the button from my coat, I decamped. An hour afterwards, in passing the same garden on rny way home, I heard Coleridge's voice; and, on looking in, there he was, with closed eyes, the button in his fingers, and the right hand gracefully waving just as when I left him. He had never missed me !—" The World of Wit and Humour."

MORT POLIOEHEN'S NERVOUSNESS.

"I don't know whether you are aware of the reason why policemen have, as a body, so strong an antipathy to wearing the silent hoots which it was at one time proposed should be worn by all-night duty men ?" said a police surgeon. " The reason is that the officers can scarcely hear their own footl ails, and this increases to a most considerable extend the nervousness which scores of men on night duty periodically suffer from. The long silence of the night, toe constant state of being on the alert, or ot endeavouring to keep so—these have a most trying effect cu the nerves (it even the strongest men, especially when the duty is in elnlet suburban neighbour- hoods. some of the men have, under such circum- stances, described the effect of silent boots as simply maddening," though the, utility of these boots is gener- ally admitted. If a man on night duty have, a predis- position to be neivons, he is soon unfit for duty, and I have had great powerful-looking young men under my charge who have said that every shadow and every unaccountable sound have reached their nerves. It seems strange that men who have been walking about all night should suffer from insomnia.. but amongst young hands who have been on night duty it is common when the time of rest comes. Rut the satirical saving that policemen • like noisy boots because the latter keep them awake,' has some scientific troth in it after all.— " Cassell's Saturday Journal."
