TOWN COUNCIL NOTES.

Everybody—at least everybody who was at all interested in the matter—was agreed that the meet-. jug of the Town Council would be over in twenty minutes on Tuesday night, last week or at least, just to allow an outside es- timate for emergencies, it was thought that half-an-hour would be ample time in which to transact all the business on the agenda paper. Yet in making these calculations one important factor had been left out of account. It had been for- gotten that the Town Council is now a much more effective talking machine than it used to be. Within the last two or three years its debating capacity has been strengthened—or shall we say lengthened? The younger members on the whole show a decided tendency to monopolise at least a fair share of the discussions, while in one or two instances older hands who were at one time pretty consistent members of the silent brigade have become unex- pectedly loquacious. The result is that when a de- bate is once started there is no lack of ability to keep it going, and therefore it is never safe to pro- phesy with regard to the length of the meetings unless you know.

The opening proceedings on Tuesday last week seemed to augur a quick despatch of business. The Mayor had no communications to lay before the meeting—not even one from the Ratepayers' Association—and the only preliminary announcements were formal intimations of the election of auditors and county councillors. The minutes of several of the commit- tees were then • rapidly disposed of, the only inter- ruption being when the Health Committee's pro- posal to close a large number of insanitary houses came under notice. Councillor Shaw Green suggested that when the notices of the insanitary condition of their houses were sent to owners there should also be an intimation of what repairs were necessary to put the houses into habitable condition. Alderman Harrison sympathised with these senti- ments, as to his "amazement and disappointment " some of his own property was included in the scheduled houses although he had spent some £6OO in eighteen months upon the fifty-three houses he owned in Warrington. The Council did not, how- ever, lend a sympathetic ear to these complaints of distressed property-owners, and Alderman Bolton rather bluntly informed Alderman "Jamison that members of the Council should not deal with busi- ness in which they were pecuniarily interested.

The debate of the evening really sprang out of the proposal of the Town Hall, Park and Baths Com- mittee to pull down the remainder of the old park wall in Sankey-street and to put in a new en- trance at the Crosfield-street corner. The debate, though it was hardly expected,answered a very use- ful purpose as it, brought out a good deal of addi- tional information with regard to the costs of the Sankey-street improvements. The proposal to pro- ceed with the additional improveraents at once had a decidedly mixed reception. The whole of the Council seemed agreed upon the point that the re- mainder of the whole wall would have to be re- moved sooner or later, but several members were strongly of opinion that this was a piece of work that might wait till something more pressing had been dealt with. Councillor Shaw-Green, as Chair- man of the Finance Committee and therefore in a certain sense the Chancellor of the Exchequer of the Council was strongly of opinion that it would be a mistake to increase the rate of this year and next by a half-penny in the pound for this improve- meat. seeing that there were so many other impor- tant improvements which would have to be under- taken. Councillor Roberts made the very reason- able request that they should have the full estimate of the cost of the improvement before they em- barked upon it,especially as they had already found from experience that the estimate of the cost of removing the other part of the wall had been greatly exceeded. The estimate that was now sub- mitted amounted to .£6450, but what about the mak- ing of the street and the re-arranging of the garden behind the new fence? Would all this not really bring up the expenditure to something like double the £650 ? A. partial answer to these questions was given later on in the meeting,when it waS admitted that another £3OO would have to be spent upon street making, bringing the total of estimated ex- penditure up to £950. It was further brought out by what was described ass kind of cross examina- tion, that while the original estimate for making the improvement in front of the Town Hall was put down at £550, the total cost had so far mounted up to £869, and,said Councillor Roberts in enumer- ating these figures " the job is not finished yet." rhe Paving and Sewerage Committee was also as- sailed for having spent .£3OO on the Sankey-street improvement without the express sanction of the Council, and the strong assault took shape in an amendment to refer back the minute of the Town Hall Committee, which was moved by Councillor Pierpomt. In moving his amendment, Mr. Pierpoint contended that there were several other matters which more urgently needed the attention of the Corporation than the Sankey-st. improvement, one of these being the provision of better accommodation for the Council itself in the Town Hall. On a vote being taken,' however, the amendment was defeated by fifteen votes to ten.

The only other subject on which any debate took place was that of the relations of the Corporation and the Ship Canal Company, and here Councillor Roberts as chairman of the Rivers' Committee had a Perfectly unanimous Council behind him. He re- ferred to the varied criticism to which the Com- mittee had been subjected. 4t bad been declared on the one hand that they were treating the Canal Company in an inconsiderate fashion, and on the other they had been accused of giving away the interests of the town to the Company. He thought that between these two extremes, the Committee might fairly conclude that they were doing the right thing. He assured the Council that nothing would be done by the committee without the knowledge and sanction of those concerned. He added the suggestion that the Corporation was getting to very close quarters with the Canal Company, and that definite action would probably be taken in a short time. Councillor R. T. Fairclough warmly repudiated the charge that the Corporation had tried to " squeeze" the Canal Company ,and mentioned several instances in which they had dealt very leniently with the Company. Alderman Harrison strongly supported the Rivers' Committee, and Councillor Beaman seemed to be of opinion that as the Corporation got little credit for their past favours to the Canal Company they might as well go in for their full " pound of flesh." Altogether it was apparent that on the matters in dispute with the Ship Canal Company the Council is of one mind.

THE ALLEGED SERIOUS ASSAULT AT WARRINGTON. ALLEGED TAMPERING WITH WIT- NESSES.

TERMINATION OF THE PROCEEDINGS.

There was a large number of people outside the War- rington Borough Police Court on Tuesday morning,when the alleged serious assault case of Broadhurst and Allen, which has so often been adjourned, was fixed to come off. At the commencement of the proceedings there were on the bench Mr. R. Garnett (chairman), Dr. Gor- nail and Mr. E.J.Guest,and Williamßroadhurst,General- st., was charged with inflicting grievous bodily harm upon Albert Edward Allen on Saturday, the 2nd of February. Mr. J. Lyon Whittle (the Town Clerk) said that the police withdrew from the case. Mr. Percy Davies appeared to prosecute on behalf of the family of Allen. Mr. Arthur Browne defended.

In opening the case Mr. Davies referred to a statement which had appeared in the newspapers and which was made by a responsible person, to wit, the Chief Con- stable who had asked for a remand at the commence- ment, and objected to bail being allowed as one of the chief witnesses had been tampered with in an improper manner. That statemeut came to the knowledge of the friends and relatives of the injured man' wh• at that andtime wat lying very seriously injured, and had been un- conscious until Tuesday morning. _ . _ .

Some discussion then arose between Mr. Davies and the Town Clerk with reference to the latter having, Mr. Davies said, attempted to obtain compensation for Allen or mitigate the charge into one of assault. Mr. Davies applied for a reinitiation of the case alto- gether and asked Allen if he charged Broadhuret with inflicting grievous bodily harm upon him. Allen : leg, sir. . .

Mr. Davies' continuing, said in fairness to himself, he should say that he had had a letter from the Town Clerk handing him the brief in the case and he protested against such conduct on tho part of the Town Clerk or any one else. He defied Mr. Whittle to deny that he [ went to Mr. Browne's office in order that a settlement might be come to. Mr. Browne denied that and said it was an absolute falsehood to say that an attempt had been made to settle the case by Mr. Whittle. Inspector Spinks, he said, came on Saturday morning with Mr. Whittle to tell him that the case was coming on. He saw Mr. Whittle first on Friday and the latter then said "are your people prepared to make any offer to the man." There was no attempt to settle the matter by a com- promise in any shape or form. Dr. G ornall : That was the initiative in the matter of a compromise. Mr. Davies : Exactly so ; I demand the letter to be read.

Mr. Whittle said Mr. Davies was making statements which were absolutely untrue. He had spoken to Mr. Browne about compensation on behalf of the family of the injured man as it was the usual thing to de, and that it would have earns weight with the. magistrates.

Mr. Davies : It was a raost -improper thing- for the Town Clerk to do.

The Town Clerk : I was appearing for the prosecutor and not for Mr. Davies or his clients.

Mr. Davies said that Mrs. Lawton, the sister of the injured man, had said that her brother had suffered a great deal of sickness t4rough the assault, and was prepared to come to court on Friday last week, and in fact Was dressed to come out for the first time when a policeman came and said the case was put off. She came to him on the Saturday morning and he ad- vised her to see the Town Clerk about it and she had done so. The Town Clerk, he said, had black- guarded her and treated her with discourtesy. He was sorry that there was such feeling, and in proceeding with the case he only wanted their worships to listen to the evidence and nothing else, and , if they thought it was a case of grievous bodily harm be did not want anything more than justice and fair play. He should like first of all that the witnesses leave the court as he had proof that they had been tampered with. The witnesses on both sides accordingly retired.

Mr. Browne then said with reference to the witnesses of whom he had five, if the charge was going to be per- sisted in he was not going to call any at all, and if the bench heard the evidence they would see that there was no case of inflicting grievous bodily harm at all. Mr. Davie,: You have:no right te say that at all Mr. Browne. He added that if their worships would listen very carefully to the evidence and take into considera- tion the character of both the prosecutor and defendant he would be satisfied. He was not going to say one word against the character of the defendant. They had both worked at Messrs. Rylands' Bros. and on the 2nd of February the prosecutor went, in the afternoon, to a publichouse called the " Gardeners' Arms" kept by a Mrs. Cook and was there some considerable time. He had some beer but knew perfectly well what he was doing, and when coming home by himself near the Brick-street end of Crossley-street he met the defen- dant, William Broadhurst. Lie knew him as his mate and simply said " How-do Skills'," (defendant's nick- name). The latter replied " How-do Nipper "and the prosecutor asked him if he would have a drink and they then went together to the " Ropemakers' Arms" in Crossley-street, and the prosecutor paid for defendant .4 drink there, but having occasion to go into the yard, on his return he was informed that defendant had left and prosecutor went on his way borne again. At the bottom of Crossley-street near the " Glassmakers Arms" prose- cutor met defendant and not knowing that he was of- fended with him said "Hello I what are you doing here," and defendant said " you are a nice b—fellew "and charged prosecutor with drinking his beer, further threatening to knock his neck out. At this juncture a man called out and asked them not "feight" and prose- cutor said " No we are not going to quarrel." This was about 6 o'clock and he then asked the defendant to come into Russell's publichouse to hate another drink, but ' the latter said he would not and thereupon butted Allen ' with his head in the mouth,with the remark " You want having a drink." Prrsecutor then fell with his head against the window-sill and thence on the ground and ' while down the defendant thumped him in the face twice. Prosecutor said " Hold on Skilly ; give us a chance ; let us get up " but the latter would not let him get up. He then became unconscious. From the date cf the assault he had only been out for the first time, that day. Mr. Davies said that Mrs. Harrison,a witness gave her evidence to P.C. Phillips and said that she was near Russell's beerhouse when she saw Allen and another man somewhat taller,fighting as described. She did not see Allen strike Broadhurst. She had also said that she saw Broadhurst kick Allen while he was on the ground. She ran after the defendant up Crossley- street but lost sight of him. The relatives of the prosecutor sent for Dr. McCarthy, who came at ',nee sr,nt for s mlnister, and also for his fri,nds and relatives, because of the condition of the man. Thinking that there was something wrong they came to him (Mr. Davies) for wsistanee. Not only had Harrison been tam- pered with, bat the doctor's is vidence had undergone a marvellous change, as he afterwards said that the ' wounds were not kicks. Mr. Davies added that the doctor's evidence w4uld show that the wound on the head could not have been caused except by a kick, unless the man had fallen from a height on to the top of his head. The calling in of a second doctor, Mr. Davies said, had been objected to by the Town Clerk, but he thought if the statements he had made were true there was strong evidence that grevious bodily harm nad been inflicted. The evidence of the witnesses was then heard, Dr. Wein thy being the first to be called.. . . .

Mary Amons, 95, Lyttigoes-lane, on the night in question was near Russell's beer-house at the corner of Crossley-street, and saw two men quarreling and saw Broadhurst strike Allen with his fist in the stomach and in conseqenee he fell to the floor. She saw no kicking and went home with Allen and stayed till 9 o'clock. John Corlett, 10 years of age, son of George Corletts 68. Be saw the defendant knock Allen's head against the window-sill.and he fell on the floor from a shove. When he was on the floor he saw Broadhurst thump -Allen and kick him somewhere about the face. Mrs. Harrison he heard shout as stated and Broadhurst then ran down Crosslerstreet. Be also never saw Allen do anything to Broadhurst.—Under cross-examination by Mr. Browne he said that some of the young women said the man had been kicked but he himself had not seen it.

Anna Rushton (11 years of age) 49, Hopwood-street, after stating the same time and place said she saw the taller ot the two men there (Broadhurst) knock Allen down and also knock his head against the window-sill. She did not see Breadburst strike Allen when on the ground. Albert Edward Allen, the prosecutor, who appeared with his nose slightly turned, said he was a labourer en- gaged at Messrs. Rvlands' and resided with his sister at 5 Derby-street, and on Saturday the 2nd of February he went to the '• Gardeners' Arms," and when he got to Brick-street at the corner of Crossley.street about four o'clock he met Broadhurst and spoke to him as Mr. Davies previously stated. At the "Gardeners' Arms" the landlady would not admit them saying they had had enough. At the " Ropemakers Arms" he went through the proceedings described and further corrcborated his solicitor. Witness in describing the assault said that defendant caught hold of is jacket and butted him in the face. When he was on the floor defendant struck him in the face. He did not know how he got the wound on the head. Defendant would not let him get up when he asked. Witness had had no quarrel with Broadhurst before.—Cross-examined by Mr. Browne he said they had been chums all their lives. He admitted having a drink or two before dinner and also that he and a young man had had some words in Mrs. Cook's public-house. He denied going to the " Manx Arms" and asking Broadhurst to help him in a fight. Prosecutor also de- nied seizing Broadhurst by the scarf at the throat and attempting to strangle him, but admitted being drunk at the time and being refused admission to the " ,Rope- makers' Arms." The prisoner's friends had supplied him with beef and fresh eggs since the occurrence, and he said that he wished the defendant no harm. He would be satisfied with compensation. It was by hies degire that Mr. Davies prosecuted. Mr. Browne said Mr. Whittle came to see him because he thought there was no case. Mr. Davies said Mr. Whittle had no right to say any- thing about the case till be heard it. Inspector Spinks said that he first heard of the matter on the Saturday night and in company with D.S. Monag- han saw Broadhurst and told him that they had come to make enquiries as there was a man seriously injured and it was alleged that he was mixed up with it more or less. Broadhurst said "No, I have not seen Allen or spoken to him since Friday. I have heard that the two men that did it went towards the barracks." Witness called again at 11.25 on Sunday night, and defendant said "What do you want now," and witness replied, " We have come for you this time." Witness told detenoant the condi- tion of prosecutor and defendant expressed a wish to see Allen; but he could not. He was afterwards charged with doing grievous bodily harm with intent to murder Allen and he replied to witness "I went to Mrs. Cook's and she refused me, and then we went to Rawlinsou's in Crossley-street. We had two pints, one each, and whilst we were in, Allen drunk another man's beer but paid for him a gill afterwards. I was taking him home afterwards when he commenced his monkey. I fait him as he was choking me. The reason I didn't tell you at first was because I did'nt think Allen was as bad as he is." Inspector Spinks said delendant was a very decent young man and had a comfortable home and a respect- able wife. He told the defendant that he would net leave a stone unturned to clear the matter from the first.

Mr. Browne said that friends would club together and offered £5 compensation. Mr. Davies lam pleased to hear it.

Mr. Garnett, the only magistrate left after a three hours' sitting, said he did not think there was any prima facie case. There was, he said, no doubt that Broad- hurst had had a very narrow escape. If it had turned out in death there would have been evidence found to take the case to the Assizes. He hoped it would be a lesson to both of them for life and advised them to take the pledge. The case was then dismissed, the parties to make their own terms. . .

Mr. Whittle said that Mr. Davies in openimg the case said he (Mr. Whittle) had attempted to stop the proceed- ings. He had, in fact, consulted the magistrates' clerk in the matter and he also thought there was no case and on going over the evidence he came to the same conclu- sion. Inspector Spinks coming to see him on Saturday morning he went to see Mr. White (the magistrates' clerk). They thought compensation was necessary and he went to Mr. Browne's office to see if his client would be prepared to pay compensation. With reference to ' blackguarding the woman he never blackguardect any- body, but only spoke seriously to her. It was not necessary to have two solicitors or two medical men in the prosecution. Mr. Davies in answer to what Mr. Whittle had said was never called on to explain about the matter until he heard it was in Court, and it was brought about by the remarks of the Chief Constable which he thought were properly made, after what had taken place that day. T 11,3 statements signed by the witnesses were entirely different from those given in the box. He Old the Town Clerk that the family wanted him to prosecute and he had allowed other solicitor + to prosecdte, and when the Town Clerk had been beholden to him in the way he had, he thought he was the last man to refuse. He was satisfied that the witnesses had been "got at" and he said it advisedly. Broadhurst attempted a crime which was certainly more than a common assault. Mr. Hunt (the chief constable) said he had sat there all the morning but did not want to have anything to say in the matter, except that on Saturday night, the 2nd of February, a report reached him with regard to the case and at his instruction steps were taken to get information. On Monday morning he came to hear direct that one of the chief witnesses had been tampered with, without any doubt whatever, and he had opposed bail being allowed on the strength of chat. That day there was a remand granted for three days, and at the end of that time a remand for a fortnight was granted, and alter that another remaod. He had heard of the kindness of the friends for the defendant towards the prosecutor,and as the time went on he aw every chance of the charge being reduced and something like mone- tary compensation offered. He was not surprised at what had taken place in that Court that morning.

The Court as then cleared, and on reaching the street it was seen that the interest in the ease had in- creased for the people 11211.1 am-et. could be numbered by hundreds. -

CHURCH HISTORY AT STOCKTON HEATH. Sir,—ln these progressive days,when Disestablishment is looming large on the horizon of futurity, it is of course to be expected that church " defenders " should make strenuous efforts to avert the impending catastrophe. But this surely does not warrant their trading upon popular ignorance of ecclesiastical history, to the extent of deliberately stating that which is historically false. A more flagrant perversion of histery never was penned or uttered, than Mr. Westbrook's assertion that, during the reign of the Conqueror, the Bishop of Rome had no jurisdiction in this realm. If the Pope had no juriscPc- tion in England between the years 1066 and 1087, what could Lanfranc, the first Norman Archbishop of Canter- bury, have meant by speaking thus in 1072: "Is it not ingrained in the consciences of all Christians that in respect to St. Peter's successors no less than to himself they • must tremble at their threats and yield joyful acclamation to their lofty graciousness when they indulge; in all ecclesiastical matters then, at last, a dispensation is valid, when it has been approved by the judgment of the suc- cessors of Blessed Peter." The council at which the fore- going was said was held in Winton by command of Pope Alexander, and had for its object the settlement of a dis- pute which had arisen between the Archbishops of Can- terbury and York cenceruingithe primacy claimed by the former over the latter. The question was decided,on the strength of former Papal decrees, in favour of Canter- bury,and the results of this conciliar enquiry were duly communicated to the reigning Pontiff. And yet there are writers and lecturers in these fin-de•sikle days, who do nO scruple to assert that during the reign of William I. the Pope had no jurisdiction in Eugland ! That asser- tion is far from being made good by the Conqueror's re- fusal to do homage to the Bishop of ROLLA. By Papal Supremacy is meant the Pope's pre-eminent authority in viritual matters. To acknowledge and submit to this Vile king never refused. The authority to which he de- clined to bow was of an entirely different order. It must be remembered that the Roman Pontiff, besides being head of the Church was a temporal ruler,the most power- ful ef hislagelAs a temporal ruler the Norman duke re- garded hin2,when he refused to do homage for his newly acquired kingdom. He never denied that the Pope was spiritually supreme, and it is this spiritual supremacy which is an essential feature of the Catholic system, and recognition of which,coupled with submission te it ccnstitutes communion with the Roman See. England participated in this communion throug- ‘. out the reign of the Conqueror, as well as before and after.—Obediently yours,

===== 0002601_18950316_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
AUG. J. HILTON.

31, Arthur-road, North Brixton, London, S.W. larch sth, 1895.

ESTABLISHED AND ENDOWED.

- Sir,—Lord Cross reminded the assembly at the Church Defence meeting on Monday evening that he was brought up as a lawyer. In following the profession of an advocate his Lordship resorted to the advocate's device of telling just as much fact as would assist his case, and studiously suppressing everything that would tell against him. Thus, while Lord Cross may be said to have spoken the truth, he did not tell " the whole truth and nothing but the truth." He said " the church existed before there was a Parliament,and therefore the Church never was established." Now whilst there is an modicum ot truth in this statement talren as a whole, it is a monstrous perversion of the truth ,as it is entirely at variance with historical fact. The church grew up with ether portions of the constitution which may be said never to have been estanlished.and ultimately it became part and parcel of the constitution itself, though the the clergy never fully became the servants of the state as the officers of other portions of the constitution became. Parliament adopted the religion of the church as the national religion, that is, Parliament established the Church of England and made provision for its maintenence by granting tithes to the church. Royal and other influential personages granted lands to the church which in former times derived its income from the following sources :—Rent of lands, fines on renewals of leases of church estates, glebe and augmentatioa lands,tithes iow eonverted into a rent charge,surplice fees, pew rents, and other incon- siderable sources. The church, however, became such a great landowner (and it should be said that these lands were freed from charges of every kind) that Parliament positively became afraid of its power, and in the end, turned the monks adrift and disendowed the church. Even at the time of the Reformation,the church possessed one-fifth of the soil of the kingdom, and at the present day it obtains its finances from land, *ad tithe rent charges. When the feud with the Pope was at an end, and the Church of England became Protestant, legal provision was made in the days of Elizabeth for the maintenance of the national religions and Parliament has ever since taken a fatherly interest in the church, which it thus established and endowed, When Queen Anne was on the throne, the woeful condi- tion of the poor clergy was represented to her with such *loving effect that she made over to the church some 270,000 which had formerly been annually paid to the Pope, and which, since the monarch had become the head of the Church, had flowed into the Queen's Trea- sury. To this large endowment, Parliament voted several substantial additions, until at length, over a unillic.n pounds sterling helped to swell the sum total of what is known as Queen Anne's Bounty. Thus we see how the church has obtained its endowments, and what connection it has with the State. To say, as Churchmen do, that the Stabs does not aid the ehurch is untrue. Parliament has always exercised its right to deal with the Church,and is at perfect liberty to do so now.—Yours, kc.

A STUDENT•

CHURCH AND STATE. Sir,—The upholders of the connection between Church and State afire.' that with the exception of £2,600.000 voted by Parliament, all the property the E,tabhshed Church now enjoys has been given to the Church by generous but mysterious "pious ancestors," and that with this property the State has no concern. This is an utter fallacy. That the Church has been,and is,subject to the control of the State, is proved by numerous Statutes. is an establishment she never had any prop- erty in her own right. She has held, and now holds her possessions from the State,and from the State alone. She has been, and is, subject.' to the rulings of the State, and not to ally "pious ancestors' " will. Kings, Dulls, and Commons in the days of Edward VI. turned out Ro- man Catholicism,as the State paid ecclesiastical system. Queens, Lords, and Commons brought it in again in the days of Queen Mary. Queen Elizabeth re-instated Pro- testant episcopacy, and, later, Parliament unceremoni- ously ousted it and established Presbyterianism. Charles 11. restored episcopacy, and the Protestant Episcopal Church obtained the endowments again. But these en- dowments were aot hers any more than they belonmi to Presbyterians, her immediate predecessors. They belonged to the State as the State, and, had the Commonwealth lasted a little longer, there is no saying but what a fresbyterian Church of England might have been at the present moment the State Church, and the episcopal Protestant Church of England a very secondary form of religious worship ! Would that have been agreeable to the Church and State defenders? The establishment of the Church of England for the third ime was nothing more than an accident. That Miura as an establishment, is capable of being unmade by the same power that created her. To say the State has provided only £2,600,000 for the Church,is to talk nonsense. To prove thislan examination must be made into the State"sops'lhat have fallen to the lot of the clergy in the rnetropolis,who have been and are a highly favoured race of ecclesiastics. Ninety-seven churches in the metropolis by an Act in the 37th reign of Henry VIII. received in 121 years, by the levying of a rate of 25.9 d. in the £ on rentals out of the perple the sum of £8.215,900. That Church is not State paid. Oh, no. I will give you an instance in Warrington. St. Paul's Church, in Bewsey-st., Warrington, was conse- crated in 1631 Of this church the Rev. 3. D. Massing- ham was appointed' incumbent in 1863. This rev gentle- Inan in Holy Trinity schools, Over Darwen, on the Ist rebruary, 1876, denied the allegation that the clergy

were State paid. He was paid apart from the State,and if anyone could show him that he received State pay,he would divide it with him. The "pious ancestor "did not defray the cost of the building of St. Paul's. The structure cost £5,489, and in August, 1831, a grant of £4,239 of State money was made,and here they go spout log up and down denying the fact. These are facts that cannot be denied. fly all means let us disestablish the Church and put all churchls and chapels on an equal footing. Let us have religious equality by all means.— Thanking you in anticipation, yours truly, J. N... CLAYTON. 54, Padgate-lane, Warrington.

CHURCH HISTORY AT STOCKTON HEATH.— " THE CASE OF MAGNA CHARTA." Sir,—Oae among the multitude of "arguments," used by the "Church defending "party, in. these criti- cal times, is that the "Church of England" was de- clared free, in the Great Charter of Liberties. There happens to be put forth by these advocates of the Church in danger, two different versions of this pas- sage in the Charter. When they are contending against the Catholics they say that the passage meant freedom from Papal domination; but when they are I on the Church preserving stump, they tell us it meant freedom from the State. They always forget one vital truth in this consideration of Magna Charts. The Church of England spoken of by the Charter was not the crea- ture of the State which goes by that name to-day. It was a part of that great historic Catholic Church which acknowledged the supremacy of Peter and his successors in the See of Rome. It was in full communion, not only with the See of Rome, but it held the same identi- cal doctrines as were held by the whole of united Chris- tendom. The constitutional law of the land declared that the Pope had jurisdiction over all in spirituals, and that the crown had none. But that was said of the true Catholic Church and not of the " Church created by the State" The freedom conceded by the Charter was the freedom which the true Catholic Church of England demanded, viz., first, freedom of election ; secondly, freedom from secular jurisdiction ; thirdly, freedom of appeal to the Roman See in all ecclesiastical matters : freedom from those" arbitrary customs," against which Lanfranc, St. Anselm, and St. Thomas A Becket fought so strenuously. Be it always remembered that King John, on behalf of the Crown, was made to sign the Charter in great measure by the agency o f Stephen Langton, a cardinal of the Roman Church ! Where now is the freedom of the " English Church ?" Is freedom of election secured to the so called English Church ? Does the church elect her own bishops ? No, decidedly no ! The State is complete master over the law-Church of England, and whilst it deals out with one band a Lefroy to Norwich, it presents with the other hand a Sheepshanks to that See. The freedom of election in the State church is a legal fiction—the Dean and Chapter dare not refuse the no ninee of the Crown, so that the freedom promised in the Charter does not exist in the establishment. The ' evidence of Bracton, Chief Justice of England, exposes the fallacy of the whole church defence position,without the necessity of discussing the merits of every dispute over ecclesiastical matters during a period of some five hundred years. How people can go up and down the country, as do [these "Church defending" gentry, asserting that they represent the old Catholic Church mentioned in the great charter, is incomprehensible! They claim the endow- ments which belonged to this old Church of England, on the strength that they belong to that same old church, but Bractou says the Pope is over all in spirituals! There is one piece of evidence which is enough to drive them for ever from their false position on this subject, and that evidence is the oath taken by the prelates of the old Church of England, contrasted with the oath taken by the prelates of the new Church of England, at their con- secration. I will ask you to print them side by side, so that your readers will be able to judge for themselves, of the truth of church defence! Oath of ancient Oath of Anglican Archbishops. Prelates. "I, Robert, Archbishop "I, A. 8., Doctor in I of Canterbury, from this Divinity, now elected, hour forward, will be faith- confirmed,and consecrated tul and obedient to St. Bishop of . . . do hereby Peter, to the Holy Aposto- declare that your Majesty lic ioman Church, to my • is the only supreme gover- Lord Pope Celestine, and his nor of this realm in spiri- successors, canonically sue- tual and ecclesiastical ceeding. I will Bovine my things. . . . . I ack- order, give aid to defend and nowledge that I hold the to maintain against every said bishopric, as well as man the primacy of the the spirttualittes and Roman Church and the temporalities thereof,only royalty of St. Peter. . . I from your Majesty," &c. will visit the thresholds of the Apostles every three years, either in person m• by my deputy, unless I be absolved by Apostolic dis- pensation. . . . . So help me God and these holy Gospels." Wilkins: 11. p. 199. Collier: Vol. I. p. 636. Oath taken also by Arch- bishop Ceolneth, A.D. 830. Oh continuity! thy natne is gammon.—Yours, &c., HIST° R ICUS.

===== 0002601_18950316_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
SOCIALISM; IS IT ROBBERY?

Being a constant reader of your paper, and reading the letters on the above subject, and daring to differ in opinion from the great autocrat,Tony Lumpkin, I make bold to pen a few theughts,for the perusal of your intelli- gent readers,not in the least expecting to broaden Tony Lumpkin's views on human life and its social surround- ings. Now, sir, touching the phrase, " nationalisation of land "T. L. says, using his own words " now, it is quite true—undeniably correct—to say that over and over again the land we love and live upon has been stolen—wrested by violence—from its previous posses- sors." These are Tony's own words, not the glib sav- ings of a " mad Socialist," but the wise words of the great Tony Lumpkin, and herein is where I must agree with the Great I Am. This being a fact, I would like to ask how long does it take—what length of time must elapse, to make stolen goods—honourable and honest commercial commodities ? How long does it take for what was wrong to grow into a right ? It what rate per annum do invalid claims become valid ? If a title gets perfect in a thousand years, how much more than perfect in two thousand years ? Coming to the point, there are two propositions before us—one is private ownership, the, other is land for Lilo people. T. L. admits the fact that private ownership has been brought about by robisery and violence, and because the Socialists &aim that this is so, and desire the people to take it back, and use it gaily for the benefit of the State, 'Pony Lumpkia dares forsooth quote scripture in condemnation of their desire, saying "Cursed is the man who removeth his neighbour's land- mark, and how deeply and doubly damned must he be who not only removes tho marks but the land also." Why, Tony, you curse your own idols, for it is the land- lords who have not only taken the marks but the land also. It is very evident, sir, from T. L.'s position and reasoning that private ownership in land is quite equit- able and just. What then is the result ? The following problem is the net outcome: If one portion of the earth's surface may justly bee.ome the possession of an individ- ual, and may be held by him for his sole us, and benefit as a thing to which he has an mrclusive right, then other portions of the earth's surface may be so held, and eventually the whole of the earth may be so, and our planet may thus lapse altogether into private hands. And mark the dilemma to which this leads. Supposing the entire globe to be thus inclosed, it follows that if the landlords have a valid right to its surface, all who are not landowners have ne right at all to live thereon ; hence such can exist on the earth by sufferance only, they are all trespassers, save by the permission of the lords of the soil. Nay, should the landlords think fit to deny them a resting place, these landless men might equitably be expelled from the earth altogether. It is manifest then, that an exclusive possession of the soil necessitates an infringement of the law of equal free dom, for men who cannot live and move and have their being without the leave of others, cannot be equally free with those others. Passing from the consideration of the possible to that of the actual, we find yet further reason to deny the rectitude of property in land. It can never be pre- tended that the existing titles to such property are legitimate. Violence, fraud, the prerogative of force, the claims of superior cunning—these are the original deeds, written with the sword rather than with the pen, not lawyers but soldiers. Blows were given for payment, and for seals blood was used in preference to wax. Just a word in conclusion, Mr. Editor. Ido not want a battle of words with Tony Lumpkin, but I am open for a hard,moraLand logical discussion, not throwing of mud and slime and shouting names. The hope that a clearer insight may be gained into the great problem of poverty in our midst, is the desire of yours respectfully, ROSEBUD.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MARCH 16 1895

SOCIALISM MEANS THE WORLD FOR TI

WORKERS.

" Honi snit qui mal y petie."

Sir,—After very carefully wading through what pur- ports to be a letter from Tony Lumpkin in your last iseue,l most say that I found in it a deal of coarse abuse without a single word of sound argument adduced. I do not propose to resort to Billingsgate language and mud-throwing, but I am open to argue every point of the Social Democratic Federation programme through the Press or on the platform with and against all corners, Tony Lumpkin included. Individualism has had an innings.has been tried and found wanting. Even Tony Lumpkin, I presume,would confess that all is not well with the present state of affairs the world over. A remedy to the existing evils is what is wanted, and the socialikic programme if carried out would cure all and every social evil now existing. Why not every honest man in these realms try and help all he can to save the country from the utter ruin and anarchy it is running to ? I wish to cut it short to-day,and as I have no time to write much, I will simplrgive you some instances of

nroven facts of robbery perpetrated in out ' midst in the light of day. The first is an extract from " Justice," Saturday, March 2nd : " The cotton duties in India passed the House of Com- mons by an overwhelming majority after an ab.urd scare had been got up by the Liberal papers that the , Government was in danger! We shall ere long have the most serious reasons to consider the economic con- dition of India. As the writer of this note has pointed out for the last eighteen years, the bankruptcy of our great dependency is inevitable. It is now close at hand. More than that, it would have been proclaimed last year if a loan of £10,000,000 in gold had not been contracted in England, to pay interest, home charges, etc. For our part the sooner forced repudiation comes the better we shall be pleased. The continuance of the present system which drains the very life-blood out of British India for the sole benefit of interest receivers, officials and ex-officials at home is nothing short of imfamous. A general revolt of the natives in euch circumstances would not only be justifiable but highly laudable. The worst of it is all of the men who have been at the head of the Indian Treasury know, and have admitted, in their confidential reports that this awful drain of over £2,000,000 a year reckoned in gold, and now about £50,000,000 reckoned in silver, means and must mean the utter ruin of the country. But they keep silence and pocket their possessions at home. In the face of the catastrophe impending the cotton duties business is a silly affair." Second, la 4 week's" Truth," brought out a very gross abuse about Lord Dudley's mining rights. By an act obtained by one of his ancestors over a hundred years ago, the Earl has the right of mining over the whole of rensett chase near Boreslev Hill, without liability for damage to property on the surface. The result is that in district after district, houses have been ruined ; and just lately between thirty and forty people in Quarry Banks have received notice to quit their houses pending mining operations, which are bound to bring about their I collapse. "' Those houses be it observed do not belong to Lord Daley, In many instances they are the free- hold property of the actual occupiers and represent the savings of a life-time. Not one ot these poor free-holders, hewever, is entitled to a penny compensation." Third, at the last sitting of the Camberwell 'Vestry the agenda paper contained a statment by Dr. F. L. Teed,the Dublin analyst,to the effect that 28 samples of milk t Ikea in the last quarter, 13 or 45-4 per cent, were adulter- ated. The same authority states in the last annual re- port that the people of Camberwell pay £lOO,OOO per annum far water in their milk, and ascribes the high rate of infant mortality to low nutrition consequent upon the infants being fed with it. The matter was re- ferred to the Sanitary Committee. All these dishonest proceedings are being carried on all round by the" thrifty 'individualists "and Tony ap- proves of the system and is afraid of a change. All we Socialists ask for is " Dieu at notre Droit."—Yours :raternally,

A. BROUCHEMITE.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

CYCLINIII. The natural impatience of the members of the War- rington Cycling Club to vnake a start in the season's runs is likely to be allayed to-day, when a run has been arranged for,to Crouton. The fulfilment of the arrange- ment, of course, depends upon the weather, but if the God of rain is in the ascendancy the committee have made arrangements for the members to go by 'bus. For the forthcoming season inter-club runs have been arranged with the Manchester Wheelers and Ramblers, Sale C.C., Widnes C.C., and Longsight B.C. The Good Friday run is to Rawarden, though a number of cyclists will probably go en tour in North Wales,where the favourite resorts are Llangollen, Berwyn, Barmouth and Dolgelly. From the commencement of the season the only thing necessary for the full enjoyment of a 1 successful summer's programme is fine weather, and if hopes and wishes have any influence that is ensored.

FOOTBALL. The Warrington football team has not covered itself with glory during the present week. Successive defeats in four days by Wigan and Rochdale Hornets are not very encouraging. It is getting very disheartening to have to report week after week these defeats, all the more so when it is considered that Warrington have al- most invariably as much of the game as their oppon- ents. Lack of finish is the factor which contributes to this result. Could the players combine better And seize chances of scoring, a different tale would be told. The defence is all right, and it is only the attacking power which is wanted. I

--- Since the present year came in Warrington has not won a match, but none of these defeats have been run- away one, which proves the assertion that the defence is good. The heaviest defeat was by Rochdale St. Clements, who won by a goal and a try to nil, but tkis result was largely due to the inclusion of Bowen. The other matches played this year were Salford who won by the goal kick, Wigan, who won by a dropped goal, and Rochdale Hornets, who won by a try. Such narrow defeats as these make them all the more tantalising.

It was admitted on all sides that the match with Wigan on Saturday, was one of the best ever seen between the two clubs. There was an almost total absence of that feeling which tends to spoil the game. Matches with Wigan are usually expected to be a long wearisome series of scrimmages, but the game on Satur- day was as open as anyone could wish. Wigan were very fortunate in winning the toss, for they had a very strong wind behind them in the first half, but which moderated greatly in the second half. Notwith- standing the assistance of the wind, all Wigan could do was to drop a goal, which, however, was sufficient to win them the game. In the second half, in addition to losing the help of the wind, the rain commenced to some down in torrents, and therefore good play was out of the question, and the ganse became nothing but a scramble. It was almost an impossibility to get away with the ball, so that Warrington failed to equalise.

Boscow kept up his form at the match, and saved his side repeatedly. Among the three-quarters, Barber and Burton put in some capital work, and had cruel luck several times. Ileesonn was not equal to his usual form, and how he missed dropping a goal in the second half and thus saving the match, is a mystery. Massey did not come up to expectations. He had too •nuch difficulty in getting away but once started he required some stopping. Bate and Houghton were quite equal to Lialliwell and Mitchinson, while there was not much to choose between forivards,Wigan being slightly the better. Takee all round, it cannot be denied that four points about represents Wigan's superiority, but had Warring- ton won the toss, it is probable that the boot would have been on the other leg.

Warrington had a capital chance of bagging two points at the expense of Rochdale Hornets on Tuesday,as that teain is at present in difficulties owing to loss of players. Intead of gaining the points, however, the Hornets got them instead, and now Warrington have only Broughton Rangers below them in the league table. They are however relatively in a better position than either Swinton or Rochdale Hornets, ,considering the number of matches played.

A draw would more fairly have represented the match at Rochdale, for the teams were vere eve„-ily matched. The solitary try gained by the Hornets was.: ibtained less than a minute before the whistle blew for half time. The ground was in a very bad condition, and was altogether against good play. Had Barber played howefer, it is very possible that the game would have resulted other- wise than it did, for there were many chances on the wing he usually plays, in which a quick runner could ' have dashed in easily. The department in which War- rington were inferior to the Hornets, was the forward, a department which in former years was their strong point, but now that the old players are dropping out, it is ex ceedingly difficult to fill their places.

The result of the League competition up to the present date is shown in the following table. Per. Apt. Pid. Won. Lost. Den. G.T. 111.T.Pts. The A team reversed the result of the previous match with Wigan. When the Wigan juniors came to War- rington, they went back in a joyous mood, having been victorious over the team which used to be the best A team in Lancashire. Warrington were able to treat the Wigan Ato the same dish on Saturday, when they visited Wigan. In these two matches, the rather rare occurrence has taken place, of each team being victori- ous on its opponents' ground.

E. New- man, and adopted. It was stated that the next meeting of the Convention would be held at Earles- -town, and a business committee was appointed to make the necessary arrangements. SONS OF TEMPERANCE:

On Wednesday evening the weal fortnightly meeting of the Samuel Morley Section of cadets was held in the Co-operative Hall, Earlestown. Bro. Gibbs, W.A., presided. After opening, and other business, readings were given by Bros. Pearson and Pickett,. At 8 o'clock the Britain's Need Division met in the same room, Bro. H. Jenkins, W.A., pre- sided in place of the Worthy Patriarch, who was prevented through sickness from being present. The Secretary in a few remarks on the good of the Order briefly showed the advantages of the Order and the security of its funds,the better benefits the members have than other societies, and he stated that the Sons of Temperance was the safest and best Sick Society in the district, and advised all young men to join a growing Society. He expected .ClOO, but never thought they would realise so much as they had. (near, hear.) DEATH OF Nina. E. LOMAS, WARGRAVE.

It is with regret that we have this week to record the death of Mrs. Elizabeth Lomas, of Wargrave- road, Earlestown, wife of Mr. Robert Lomas, who for over 23 years held the appointment of deacon at the Congregational Church, Newton. The death occurred in the presence of her relatives on Monday morning last, after an illness of not more than six days. She had previously suffered from an attack of influenza, from which it was thought she had reco% ered, brit a relapse supervening, inflammation and pleurisy set in, unfortunately with fatal issues, at the age of 64 years. The deceased lady, who was held in great respect by her neighbours and acquaintances was the eldest daughter of the late Mr. Thomas Roberts of Ruthin, North Wales, who was one of the first detachment of workmen who came to Earlestown when the L. and N.-W.Co., took over the Viaduct Wagon Works. She was ene of the oldest residents in the town, having resided there something like 42 or 43 years. She had been a member of the mother's meeting held in Earles- town under the presidency of Mrs. Evans, of Hay- dock, for upwards of 20 years and was held in much esteem by her fellow members. She was also a member of the Congregational church for 35 years, and for many years was a useful worker in connec- tion with the church. Her loss will be greatly felt, not only by her husband, hut by the whole of her large circle of relatives. She was a woman of very even temperament, and the respect in which she was held was shown by the large number of floral wreaths which were placed on the coffin. The ,funeral took place on Thursday afternoon at the Congregational Church, Newton, the officiating minister being the Rev. James Allatt. A short and impressive service was conducted at the house by Mr. Allatt, and besides the immediate relatives of the deceased, the mem- bers a the mother's meeting attended to pay a last tribute of respect to the mentory of the deceased. The coffin was of polished oak, with solid brass mountings, and engraved plate bearing the inscrip- tion "Elizabeth Lomas, died March 11; 1895, aged 64 years." A large artificial wreath was sent by the deceased's husband and Mr. and Mrs. P. Roberts; other wreaths were sent by Mr. and Mrs. John Roberts, Misses Roberts (Manchester), Mrs. A. Roberts (Goole), Mr. and Mrs. Duncalf, Mr. and Mrs. Atkinson, Mr. and Mrs. Gilmour (Golborne), Mr. and Mrs. J. U. Walton, Mr. and Mrs. C. 11. Bod- mer, Dr. Patterson, and Mrs. Pritchard. The funeral arrangements were most efficiently carried out by Messrs. Evans and McClure of Earlestown.9l STRANGE CONDUCT OF A DRUNIZN MAN. A peculiar find was made by a Runcorn green- grocer named Mr. William Ackerley. early on Wed- nesday morning, when proceeding to his father's farm at Astmere, When nearing the bridge cross- ing the Bridgewater Canal at this village he saw a heap of clothing which on examination he found to consist of a pair of trousers, pair of shoes, cardigan jacket, pair of stockings and a scarf. He took pos- session of them and on returning to Runcorn,handed them over to the police. It was at once concluded that the owner of the clothing must have com- mitted suicide, but on investigation being made it was ascertained that it was a drunken freak. The person to whom the clothes belonged had partially undressed himself in t'le roadway, thinking himself at home. He fell asleep on same mossy bank hard ' by,and when he awakened he was unable to find his clothing. As may be imagined he had a very un- comfortable and nneviable walk,in his scanty attire to his house in the neighbonrhood of Clarence Ter- race, Runcorn.

THE INDEPENDENT LABOUR PARTY.

(BY Mn. H. WHITEA.M.) If I venture to say a few words reflecting upon the wisdom of the advanced Radicals in forming a _ _ .

new party, I hope to be pardoned. It is surely clear to such that no mere geographical lines can be drawn in the world that the political or sociolo- . _ . .

gical idealist has fondly sketched in his imagina- tive brain. In plain English I want no one to say "Mind your own business Harry and we will mind ours." For myself I have seen for some years that the Labour Party is one, a simple unit. No matter what colour, sr what _nationality the competitive

system has forced us all into one camp. The great pity is, we cannot see this and act accordingly. Let me be understood here. While believing that

the Labour Party was never more severely p—ushed than it is to-day, 1 believe the same also applies to those whom we are in the habit of looking upon as capitalists, or, in other words, let us say our em-

ployers. The ordinary trade unionist preaches combina- tion and nothing but combination. He feels to have quite a heap of saintship about him when he gives forth the somewhat misleading phrase, " De- ience not Defiance." If he does once now and again see a glimmering of light and fancy it may be worth while to look into the ethics that are

supposed to be leading us on to a higher life, it will certaintly be as the tail end supporter of a party that has no real sympathy with trade union- ism, a party that has seen the utter inadequacy of meeting new conditions with old and obsolete methods.

Reform is now the watchword throughout the civilized world. It is seen that we are only wasting our time fignting capital with strikes and the like worn-out weapons. The law of evolution must teach us how to seize hold of the means it carries with it from age to age for the eradication of any disease afflicting the body politic. Capital is itself engaged in a death struggle. The small tradesman and the small manufacturer are being gradually, yet surely crushed out. A householder can now trade with one business firm. Everything he needs no matter what it may be—food, raiment or mental pabulum, may be had at the lowest cost, and yield- ing the smallest profit to the head, or heads, of the firm, yet enable them to grow rich. Two features stand out prominent, and cause the thinking man some trouble of mind. These are the centreing of the wealth of the world in the hands of a few, and the ever increasing amount of misery, dire misery. We might fairly add to these glaring features of every day life, that increasing amount of crime that has its source in the fertile and well educated brain of the intelligent man. Nothing has struck me so much during the past twenty years as the steady increase of crime among the class whom we of the proletariat are in the habit of looking up to as our pastors and masters. Is it a fact indeed that the prophecy has come out true, and that we are, nothing but educated devils ? The leading criminal cases, as reported in the daily press, are, in every description of court, those that are bred in the homes of the rich, and the oftces of those struggling to become so. The poor have no respect for the law nor the judge who administers it. They know the one is merely used by the other simply to keep we poor wretches in order. Yet all are not bad, we have not entirely gone, warp and woof to the devil, No, and the fact of this may be seen in the many efforts now being put forth to better the condition of things. Day by day we gain some little experience, and try, over a talk with our fellows, to give it practical effect. Out of this springs some new movement. A few cranks meet in some corner of Tooley-street, and at once declare that we, "the people of England have determined to down with everything." Some of us are tryisig to substitute universalism for individualism believing it to be the only ground principle. Possibly we are making a mistake in trying to alter the conditions of life, before alter- ing the morale of the man. Some of us are not satis- fied to go along any longer with the party that has had many watch words. Out of the word reform has sprung quite a numerous and illustrious pro- geny, not, the least of which has been the one which the readers of the " Examiner " have been proud to march under, and that is the word " Organize." Yes, we have known that we were right, knowing that, we have been anxious to press forward, throwing out, to right, and to left, the word that carried with it magical results "Organise." With this talis- man ever before us, we have forced the timid Whig to quit sitting on the rail. The weak-kneed Libe- ral has had to stand aside or suffer being snuffed out, while the good old Tory has stood aghast at the dizzy leap in the dark a Dizzy could force him to take. This, and the history only to be found in a good library, has been done with the aid of or- ganisation. I think were I in Warrington now I should not be a member of the Independent La- bour Party. As a Socialist I should stay in the ranks of the Liberal Party trying to influence it. In trying to build up the party and press forward my views in it I think I should be doing good work. I was for years intimately acquainted in the dear old town with good Socialist, who always refused to work or vote with the Liberal Party,saying it was a waste of time. He seemed quite oblivious to the fact that he was wasting time with a vengeance by enabling a Tory to get the seat. I have myself come to see how undesirable it is for little knots of workingmen to sever themselves from a class of men who by training and opportunity are better fitted to think out and cope with the deadly enemy now stalking amongst us. Of course, I refer simply to misery. Could we see elearly the way to stop the growth of large cities,and cultivate a contented peasantry we should be doing something, at all events. Are we mentally equipped for such work? I think not. In the report of January 12th of the policy of the ILL'. as given by Mr. Gee at the Warrington De- bating Society, I find that gentleman saying, "He wished the workingmen of the country to throw off the curse of thrift." Now, I don't think Mr. Gee meant to say anything of the kind or, if he did, he certainly meant to say more. The opposite to thrift he would certainly not commend, or, if he did, he would be no acquisition in a socialist community. There is some little difference between thrift, and that grasping selfishness which is always crying out "there is no room on the top." The man who is satiAed to cheer his fellows on with the encour- hug words," Look after yourself and the devil take the hindmost " is,l think,the man Mr. Gee referred to, and he certainly wants looking after. Working- men, os a body, are not cursed with thrift. The curse goes rather the other way. And yet it is not true, not by any means,that the wealthy among us, are so as a result of thrift. Let any reader of this look around among his rich and well-to-do neigh- bours, and see how many are so in consequence of thrift. It is a well known fact that the power to exploit his fellowmen, to seize opportunity to have a cranium full of good business bumps, are the secrets that now lead a man to fortune. A man may be well posted in all the ologies, have the his- tory of the world at his fingers end, and be in fact a walking library, yet, lacking business qualities, he will run a close race with the Workhouse. We see all this, end naturally ask why it should be so no fault can be found with the man full of business resources, nor yet with the man lacking them. What we find fault with is the system that makes one man miserably wealthy and keeps the other corruptibly poor. Generally speaking thef working-man has little opportunity for thrfft. I married he has enough to do to keep those about him decent and respectable. His little luxuries may consist of a visit now and again to the theatre or buying his weekly allowance of " bacca," or an occasional glass of beer. Of course our friend, Mr. Hall, would reject all this as so much waste, yet the fact remains that a man may boa good citizen and yet be guilty of all this wastefulness. For myself, I might all my life have abjured the theatre, beer and "bocce, and yet all my thrift would have evaporated at sight of a good second-hand book store. If this did not use up the good results of thrift, a visit to the dry goods store certainly would, and I should have been well repaid in the beaming faces I should find at home when unpacking the little present of new hat or new frock. We are not all built alike. It is not fitting that we should be, and yet we may be the comple- ment of each other. A bricklayer may build a nice house. The cabinet maker may furnish it well. The artist may decorate it, and the literary man may build, furnish and decorate the man in the truest sense and fit him for the home. Can anyone say why any one of these workmen should hold his head up higher or be richer than the other? Cer- tainly not. Of course I am not dealing with the drunken spendthrift. Ile belongs to no class. Nor am I taking into account the man who, from birth to death, has been arrayed in fine linen, yet never produced anything, or made the world richer for his presence in it, nobleman though he may be called. To get back to the LL.P. it seems to me that I quite understand the motive underlying the move-

ment. A feeling of dissatisfaction is spreading among the people. It is just the same here in I America. There are two great parties, the Re- publicans and the Democrats. With neither party I are reformers satisfied, and straightway they set about building up another party, as a consequence, the whole of the political field is covered with weaklings that cut ne show at election times. I ast acknowledge that they do a deal of educa- tional work, but this could be done more effectu- ally within the lines of either party, where the machinery, and conveniences arising out of wealth, and business methods already exist. Is it neces- sary to form a new party in Warrington, keep- ing in view the strength of the Tory party ? I think not, or if so, it must result in leaving timid Liberals as a prey to the Conservatives, and a waste of time in building up that new party. We may feel dissatisfied at the pace we go at, but it is the sheerest folly to seek out caves in which we may bellow our dissatisfaction. The Conservative party years ago, had an experience of this kind, so also had the Liberal party, and the Irish party are to- day damming Home Rule by making themselves resemble John Bright's terrier dog. Wherever this famous dog may be, we want to see and hear tim distinctly, and this can only be done when he stands well to the front in the party to which he naturally belongs. The speaker whom I have already quoted,further said "Look at society to-day and compare these countries where the capitalistic system was properly developed with those where the development had not been so advanced, and they would find far more poverty in the former countries." I have read this passage over several times, and must confess my utter inability to understand it, or, if understand- ing it to reconcile it with the current history of the nations of the world. Will Mr. Gee kindly explain ? It would seem as if our good friend thinks these are , countries where the capitalistic system is " pro- perly "developed, and in this country we find far more poverty than in the country "when the ' development had not been so advanced." In L no country on the face of this earth can • it be said that capital is properly devel- oped, our friend should inform us as to mean- ing the words " Capital," " properly," and " deve- loped" carry with them. Mr. Hunter Watts has recently told the members of the S.D F. that " socialists cared little about law and order." Possibly the gentleman meant to say that it was the want of law and order, so painfully apparent in the world, that the socialist was striv- ing for. Another snialist and an M.P. said recently that he wanted fifty men in each of the large cities to agree on a given day to enter a shop, and supply himself with what he needed most, and this policy pursued for a week, would advance,Mr. Keir liardie thought, " the social revolution." All this wild talk will do the socialists no good. We must re- cognise the fact that as the world has not been built up in a day, so it cannot with safety be overthrown in a day. We must accept the natural law which is quite patent to the observer, as the seed in dying gives out new life, so that the dying capitalistic system will, ere its death struggle comes, give us the germs of a system that will make life worth

living. - I was pleased to read in the "Examiner" of January 19th the leader treating upon John Burn's visit to the States. He was pretty plain spoken while here, and managed to ruffle the feathers of the American Eagle. An effort was made to scream him down, and with some success. Thrifty and skilful workman as honest John is, he would find it next to impossible to find rest for the sole of his foot in this " sweet land of liberty." The thrifty capitalist, the man who stands by the time- honoured saying—" Can I not do as I like with my own," makes it uncommon hot for the labour leader, especially if he happens to be poor, or a member of a weak union. My friends will all know by this, that my family and I are all back in San Francisco, what I think of the city and its great and all absorbing labour question I will reserve for a future letter. I wish particularly to draw attention to my present address, as letters and papers reach me only after considerablo trouble to the P.O. Always note the address as I am liable to move ally day :-807, Page-street.

HAYDOCK.

GALLANT Rascup.--At the St. Fielens Police Court, on Monday,the Mayor (Councillor Martin),who prrsided on the Bench, presented to Canstable Edward Gresty, of Bla.ckbrook, Haydock, a tostimenial from the Royal Humane Society, London, for his efforts in restoring to life a woman named Ellen Wilcock, wife of Mark Wil- cock, of Down-green, Ashton, who, on the 28th Dec., attempted to commit suicide by throwing herself into the canal at Blackbrook. Chief Constable Wood stated the facts of the case. The Mayor, in making the pre- sentation to Constable Gresty spoke of the benefits of the instruction given by the St. John Ambulance Society, which had been the means of saving many lives. The testimonial was inscribed on vellum., and signed by the Duke of Argyll as president. Chiof Constable Wood after- wards presented another testitnouial to P.C. Dillon.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHER-WARRINGTON.

SERVICES FOR THE PEOPLE.

There was a large attendance at the People's Service held in the Bold-st. Wesleyan Church on Sunday after- noon. The Rev. G. D. Mason, who conducted the meet- ing, referred to his recent accident and said that during his temporary confinement he had been fercibly reminded of the truth that blessings were scarcely appreciated till lost. The most unfortunate beings might find much in life to be thankful for, and he urged them to cultivate I the spirit of gratitude to God for the innumerable mer- cies with which he had crowned every life. Mr. W. J. Ashton, who gave the address, referred to the folly of man in failing to appreciate the good things God had provided, and raised the question why men, convinced of the superiority of the religious life over the irreligious, paused at the threshold of its privi- leges and hesitated to accept its blessings. He proceeded to show that the objections to religion arose from a mis- conception of its nature, and a false representation of its operations,stating that the religious life was not an irksome but a bright sunny experience that the incon- sistencies of professors ware not chargeable to religion but to the absence of it, and that Jesus Christ was true and had declared that His poke was easy and His bur- den light. He said the sinner was a slave, and that the religious life was a life of liberty that they and only they whom Christ had freed were free ir2Hoed. He spoke of these people who satisfied themselves with the assurance that they were as good as others,and would fare as well as most folk. They were responsible to God, he said, not as communities, but as individuals, and that they must fare better than most folk,for it would be of little avail for them to attempt to conceal their defects behind another person's vices. Several selections of music were remarkably well rendered by the orchestra, and "There is a green hill" was sung in an excellent manner by Mr. Levi Richardson.

THE TORY LEADERS AND THE EIGHT

HOURS BILL.

DEPUTATION FROM THE MINERS' FEDERATION. The Marquis of Salisbury and Mr. Balfour received on Thursday afternoon, at the reaidence of the former, in Arlington-st., a deputation from the :Miners' Federation who were headed by Mr. Pickard, M.P., and Mr. S.

Woods. M.P. Mr. Pickard said tbe Miners' Eight Hours Bill, which they advocated, was not a party measure and many Conservative miners, especially in Lancashire, thought the Conservative leaders should have an opportunity of pronsuncing upon it. In Parliamentary elections the miners voted for those candidates who expressed them- selves strongly in favour of the Miners' Eight Hours Bill, and in Parliament the measure had friends on both sides. _

Mr. Aspinwall followed, and stated that thousands of Conservative miners in Lancashire strongly favoured the bill. He repudiated the idea that the passing of the bill would at all materially interfere with the cost of production or the course of trade in the country. After Mr. Haslam, Derbyshire, had also spoken. Mr. Balfour replied that as this was not a question of party polities it could not be dealt with by the party organisation, and he had no title to speak for his own party upon it. There were certain difficulties connected . with the subject which had always prevented him from supporting the Eight Hours Bill in the House of Commons. Its Om- pulsory enactment would dislocate the whole Eptem in Northumberland and Durham, where the shifts of work- Zng coal were different from other parts of the country. Such a dislocation would be prejudicial to all concerned in the industry, He was strongly in favour of dimin- ished hours of work so far as that increased the effi- ciency of the worker, but the present movement was ex- pected by some of its friends to go further than that. Whilst his mind was open to every argument,there were weighty difficulties which prevented him promising them his support. Lord Salisbury, who also briefly replied, discouraged a suggestion of Mr. Pickard that the measure might be introduced in the Upper House. The House of Commons was more capable of dealing in the first instance with such a measure, and the action of the Peers must depend largely upon the extent and nature of the support the bill received there, and the character of the arguments pleaded for it.

RUNCORN UNION FEMALE NIGHT NURSE. The Guardians require a night Nurse for the Work- house Hospital at Dutton. Applicants must be between 25 and 40 years of age, and must be single women or widows without children depec dent upon them. They must have received a training in General Nursing and be qualified to undertake Midwifery cities The person appointed will be required to take night or day duty at the discretion of the Medical Officer and the Matron. She will be required to perform the duties of the office of Nurse as :prescribed by the Local Government Board and generally to observe all orders and directions of the Medical Officer, and it net be willing to assist in the General management of the House under the control of the Master and Matron. Salary 50 per annum with supper and breakfast in the House but no lodging or day accommodation will be provided except on an exchange of duties with the Day Nurse when day accommodation and dinner and tea in lieu of supper and breakfast will be provided. Applications in the handwriting of the Candidates, stating age. present And past position aud Hospital experience, accompanied by copies of testimonials of recent date (not ex- ceeding three in number) te be sent to me on or before the 26th instant. Further particulars may be known on applica- tion to the Matron in the Workhouse. The appointment will be made on the 2ad April 1895, and selected Candidates will receive notice to attend on that day, and third class return fare will be paid. By Order. JOHN ASHTON, Clerk to the Guardians. Runcorn 14th March 1F95. a

footman. "You wilt step this way to my lady's boudoir." Doris followed him through the hall, with its crimson velvet carpets and handsome chairs up the broad staircase, where the flowers and statues gave the place an aspect of fairyland. She saw before her a long, broad corridor where hung some superb pictures. The man drew aside a heavy velvet curtain, and she was in Mrs. Long - worth's boudoir, in the presence of that lady her- self. ".Miss Brandon, who wishes to apply for the governets's position," announced the footman. Doris stepped timidly forward, her face flushing under the sharp scrutiny 'of the keen, cold grey eyes bent upon her. "Really," said Mrs. Longworth, indicating seat with a haughty wave of her jewelled hand. "Can it be possible you are applying for my situa- tion ? ' Doris modestly answered: "Yes, madame." "You are so young to teach my child—scarcely more than a child yourself." "I am seventeen," replied Doris, in a low, sweet voice. "What are your accomplishments, young lady? I suppose you have a thorough knowledge of the languages, music, drawing, etc. I should like to hear you read. Ah, here is a French novel, and in French. This will do." - Doris took up the book, and her clear, well- modulated accents rather pleased the lady. "I have but one child—a little daughter," she said; "and as she is destined to be more than ordinarily plain-looking, I should like her to be clever and accomplished. Will you read me a little Italian? Then you may sit down at the piano and play a few bars of something." And Doris read again, the sweet, soft Italian falling like clear notes from her lips. Doris confessed to not being very well accomplished in music. "That will do. And now about the remunera- tion expected. Taking into consideration the fact that I shall have to pay expensive masters for the branches in which you are deficient, I thought of offering you one hundred dollars a year." Less—much less—than she paid to the humblest aervant in her house. The cook would have laughed at such a sum; the housemaids have in- dignantly refused it; the footmen have considered themselves insulted at such an offer; but Doris, ignorant of all this, thought it a magnificent awn, indeed. "A hundred dollars a year !" she repeated. Mrs. Longworth, whose conscience was not quite dead, thought she was reproaching her. "We would begin with that," she said. "If I found my child improved under your care, I would increase it. Can you come to me soon?" "At once, it you would like," replied Doris, gratefully. "There remains only one thing, and that is references. Of course, having come from the bureau, you have good ones." "I have none," replied Doris, simply. "I have no one to whom I could refer." "In that case," replied Mrs. Longworth, "please consider the interview ended;, and I shall not fail to express my indignation to the manager of the bureau for having dared to send me a person who had no reference to offer. Good morning." And with a cold bow, she turned to her book, as though to intimate she had nothing further to say. There was a burning flush on Doris's face as she passed out of the elegant mansion. must not lose heart," she said to herself. "I will try Mrs. Heath next." Again Doris found herself mounting the steps of a magnificent mansion in the fashionable portion of the city. Mrs. Heath looked up in wonder at the fair young girl ushered into her presence. Briefly Doris ' stated her errand. "I may as well state, in the first place, that I have no references to offer," thought Doris; and she acted on The impulse of the moment. • "In that case it is a pity you troubled yourself by coming here," Mrs. Heath said curtly. "You will not suit me." Doris crushed back the tears in her blue 'eyes, and sought the third address on the list. "Yes, Mrs. Dorchester is at home, Miss," said the servant, ushering Doris into a superb drawing room. "Be seated. It seemed to Dorris she must have been seated in the drawing-room quite half an hour, waiting patiently, when suddenly she heard footsteps and the rustle of a silken robe. The next moment the door opened, and an elegantly dressed lady swept into the room. She started violently as her eyes fell upon Doris's face. (To be continued). 7,ROW i HIS TO SOME WOMAN. WEEKLY REPORT ON TEE HEALTH OF WARRINGTON. Week ending 2nd March 1895. During the week, which was the 9th of the current year, the following births and deaths were registered : Births. Deaths. Warrington 3B 33 Latchford 3 2 Total 4l 35 The excess of births over deaths was 6. The death rate from all causes was equal to 61-8 per 1,000, against 213 the average of the previous four weeks, and agains6,. 19.6 the corresponding week of last year. The death - rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to 111 per 1,000, against 1.3 the average rate of the fouy previous weeks, and against 5 5 the corresponding of 1894. The rainfall measured .25 inch. The general direction was N., the reading of the anemometer denoting 1100 miles. weeK JOHN H. GORNALL, Medical Offic(r of Health.
